\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}+\cdots,

(2.102)

where the ellipsis indicates diagrams with an increasing number of mass-
m
insertions on the worldline (denoted by black circle). Curly lines represent graviton propagators.
Bulk graviton vertices are obtained by expanding the Einstein–Hilbert action
S_{\text{EH}}
(
2.8
) to cubic, quartic, and higher orders in perturbation theory around Minkowski space. In the following, we explicitly compute the first diagram in the series (
2.102
) and show that it reproduces the leading Schwarzschild correction to the background Minkowski metric. Subleading corrections in
Gm
can be obtained by evaluating the remaining diagrams in the expansion (
2.102
). Resumming the series ultimately reconstructs the full Schwarzschild metric
276
,
219
,
362
,
465
,
176
,
466
.

The first diagram of
eq.

2.102
is obtained by combining the vertex in
S_{\rm pp}
that couples
h_{\mu\nu}
to the point particle, with a graviton bulk propagator.
The Feynman rules for the point-particle coupling and the graviton propagator in de Donder (dD) gauge are, respectively,

m
\scriptstyle{\mu\nu}
\scriptstyle{k}

\displaystyle\quad=\quad i\frac{m}{M_{\mathrm{Pl}}}\int\mathrm{d}{\affine}\,e^{-ik\cdot{X}({\affine})}\frac{\mathrm{d}{X}^{\mu}}{\mathrm{d}{\affine}}\frac{\mathrm{d}{X}^{\nu}}{\mathrm{d}{\affine}}\;,

(2.103)

\scriptstyle{\rho\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\mu\nu}
\scriptstyle{k}

\displaystyle\quad\equiv\quad G^{\rm dD}_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}(k^{2})=\frac{i}{k^{2}}P_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}=-\frac{i}{2k^{2}}\left(\eta_{\mu\rho}\eta_{\nu\sigma}+\eta_{\mu\sigma}\eta_{\nu\rho}-\eta_{\mu\nu}\eta_{\rho\sigma}\right)\,,

(2.104)

from which we can compute the graviton one-point function. In the rest frame of the point particle, we have

\displaystyle\langle h_{\mu\nu}(x)\rangle

\displaystyle=-\frac{m}{M_{\mathrm{Pl}}}\int\mathrm{d}{\affine}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}{X}^{\rho}}{\mathrm{d}{\affine}}\frac{\mathrm{d}{{X}}^{\sigma}}{\mathrm{d}{{\affine}}}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\frac{e^{-ik\cdot({X}(\affine)-x)}}{k^{2}}P_{\rho\sigma\mu\nu}

(2.105)

\displaystyle=\frac{m}{M_{\mathrm{Pl}}}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}{{X}}_{\mu}}{\mathrm{d}{\affine}}\frac{\mathrm{d}{{X}}_{\nu}}{\mathrm{d}{\affine}}+\frac{1}{2}\eta_{\mu\nu}\right)\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\vec{k}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{e^{i\vec{k}\cdot\vec{x}}}{\vec{k}^{2}}\,.

(2.106)

Using the formula

\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}\vec{k}}{(2\pi)^{d}}\frac{e^{i\vec{k}\cdot\vec{x}}}{\vec{k}^{2}}=\frac{\Gamma(\frac{d}{2}-1)}{(4\pi)^{d/2}}\left(\frac{\vec{x}^{2}}{4}\right)^{1-\frac{d}{2}},

(2.107)

we obtain

\langle h_{\mu\nu}(x)\rangle=\frac{M_{\mathrm{Pl}}}{2}\frac{r_{\mathrm{s}}}{r}\left(\eta_{\mu\nu}+2\frac{\mathrm{d}{{X}}_{\mu}}{\mathrm{d}{\affine}}\frac{\mathrm{d}{{X}}_{\nu}}{\mathrm{d}{\affine}}\right),

(2.108)

where
r=\sqrt{\vec{x}^{2}}
.
Choosing
{\affine}=t
as the affine parameter, so that
{X}^{\mu}(t)=(t,\vec{0})
and
\frac{\mathrm{d}{X}^{\mu}(t)}{\mathrm{d}{\affine}}=(1,\vec{0})
,
eq.

2.108
reproduces the Schwarzschild metric in isotropic coordinates
355
,
198
up to the first non-trivial order in
G
:
46
46

46

A note of caution: here,
r
denotes the radial distance in isotropic coordinates and should not be confused with the Schwarzschild coordinate
r
that we use in
section

4
(see, e.g.,
eq.

3.1
).

\mathrm{d}s^{2}=\big(\eta_{\mu\nu}+2M_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{-1}\langle h_{\mu\nu}(x)\rangle\big)\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}\mathrm{d}x^{\nu}=-\left(1-\frac{r_{\mathrm{s}}}{r}\right)\mathrm{d}t^{2}+\left(1+\frac{r_{\mathrm{s}}}{r}\right)\mathrm{d}\vec{x}^{2}+\mathcal{O}(r_{\mathrm{s}}^{2})\,,

(2.109)

provided one identifies the parameter
m
with the ADM mass
M
, i.e.,

2Gm=2GM\equiv r_{\mathrm{s}}.

(2.110)

We stress that, for the identification
m=M
, it is crucial that the comparison between the EFT solution and the full metric linearized in
r_{\mathrm{s}}
(
2.109
) be performed in the same gauge. Alternatively, the same result can be obtained by comparing gauge-invariant quantities in the EFT and in the full theory.

Solving the equations of motion with a point source

For completeness, we note that a more mundane way of obtaining the matching (
2.110
) is to solve the Einstein equations directly in the static limit. Varying
S_{\rm EFT}=S_{\rm EH}+S_{\rm pp}
yields, in the rest frame of the point particle,

M_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{2}G_{\mu\nu}^{(1)}[h]=-m\,\delta_{\mu}^{0}\delta_{\nu}^{0}\,\delta^{(3)}(\vec{x}),

(2.111)

where
G_{\mu\nu}^{(1)}
is the linearized Einstein tensor,

G_{\mu\nu}^{(1)}[h]=-\frac{1}{M_{\mathrm{Pl}}}\left[\partial^{\rho}\partial_{\mu}h_{\nu\rho}+\partial^{\rho}\partial_{\nu}h_{\mu\rho}-\square h_{\mu\nu}-\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}h-\left(\partial^{\rho}\partial^{\sigma}h_{\rho\sigma}-\square h\right)\eta_{\mu\nu}\right].

(2.112)

We now look for static, spherically-symmetric solutions of
eq.

2.111
.
In the de Donder gauge (
2.100
), which implies
\partial^{\rho}\partial^{\sigma}h_{\rho\sigma}=\frac{1}{2}\square h
, the linearized Einstein tensor reduces to

G_{\mu\nu}^{(1)}[h]=\frac{1}{M_{\mathrm{Pl}}}\left(\square h_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\eta_{\mu\nu}\square h\right).

(2.113)

Taking the trace of
eq.

2.111
with
\eta^{\mu\nu}
and using
eq.

2.113
, one finds
M_{\mathrm{Pl}}\square h=-m\,\delta^{(3)}(\vec{x})
. Plugging this back into
eq.

2.111
gives the following equation for
h_{\mu\nu}
:

\nabla^{2}h_{\mu\nu}=-\frac{m}{M_{\mathrm{Pl}}}\left(\delta_{\mu}^{0}\delta_{\nu}^{0}+\frac{1}{2}\eta_{\mu\nu}\right)\delta^{(3)}(\vec{x}),

(2.114)

where the d’Alembert operator has been replaced by the spatial Laplacian for time-independent perturbations.
Integrating over a sphere of radius
r
, and applying the divergence theorem and spherical symmetry, one finds the following equation in
r
:
4\pi r^{2}\partial_{r}h_{\mu\nu}=-\frac{m}{M_{\mathrm{Pl}}}\left(\delta_{\mu}^{0}\delta_{\nu}^{0}+\frac{1}{2}\eta_{\mu\nu}\right)
. Dividing by
r^{2}
and integrating over
r
yields

2M_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{-1}h_{\mu\nu}=\frac{2Gm}{r}\left(2\delta_{\mu}^{0}\delta_{\nu}^{0}+\eta_{\mu\nu}\right),

(2.115)

in agreement with
eq.

2.109
, upon identifying
m=M
.

2.3.2
Tidal response from one-point function

In the previous section, we illustrated the computation of a one-point function within the EFT framework, using the action
S_{\mathrm{EH}}+S_{\mathrm{pp}}
. We now slightly modify the physical setup and assume that, in addition to the flat metric
\eta_{\mu\nu}
, the background
\bar{g}_{\mu\nu}
includes an external tidal field, which we will denote by
H_{\mu\nu}
. On this background, we again compute the one-point function of
h_{\mu\nu}
. In this case, rather than describing the gravitational potential sourced by the point particle, the one-point function represents the response induced by the external field
H_{\mu\nu}
. As before, for pedagogical clarity, we will present different equivalent methods of performing this calculation.

Tidal response from a diagrammatic approach

Let us begin with a diagrammatic approach. Starting from the EFT action (
2.9
), we are now interested in couplings to the point particle involving two graviton lines. Focusing on the static tidal response at tree level, the relevant interactions are those arising from
S_{\mathrm{h.o.}}
without time derivatives. The couplings generated by the expansion of
S_{\rm pp}
in
h_{\mu\nu}
are important for reconstructing the tidal source at subleading order in
r_{\mathrm{s}}
and for performing matching calculations at loop level (see, e.g., Refs.
389
,
358
for some examples), but they do not contribute to a tree-level calculation.
We may therefore neglect contributions from the point-particle action
S_{\rm pp}
at this stage. We will comment on how to extend the calculation to loop order at the end of this section.

As mentioned, we consider the following background metric:

\bar{g}_{\mu\nu}(x)=\eta_{\mu\nu}+H_{\mu\nu}(x)\,,

(2.116)

where
H_{\mu\nu}(x)
is assumed to be static. In particular, it solves the flat-space Einstein equations with tidal field boundary conditions at infinity, and is regular at the origin. In Fourier space, we write it at zero frequency as

H_{\mu\nu}(k)=2\pi\delta(k^{0})H_{\mu\nu}(\vec{k})\,,

(2.117)

and introduce the following diagrammatic convention:

}\quad\equiv\quad H_{\mu\nu}\,.

The advantage of working with the background field method is that the final result for
h_{\mu\nu}
is
covariant under diffeomorphisms of the external metric
\bar{g}_{\mu\nu}
.
This allows us to choose the tidal field in any convenient gauge of our choice. In what follows, we will choose
H_{\mu\nu}
to satisfy the vacuum Einstein equation on a flat background consistent with the Regge–Wheeler gauge
551
, which we discuss in
section

4
. In particular, focusing for simplicity on the gravito-electric sector,

H_{\mu\nu}(\vec{k})=\sum_{\ell,m}\mathcal{E}^{({\rm E})}_{\ell m}(\eta_{\mu\nu}+2u_{\mu}u_{\nu})i^{\ell}{\cal Y}_{\ell m}^{i_{1}\cdots i_{\ell}}\frac{\partial}{\partial k^{i_{1}}}\cdots\frac{\partial}{\partial k^{i_{\ell}}}(2\pi)^{3}\delta{}^{(3)}(\vec{k})\,,

(2.118)

where
\mathcal{E}^{({\rm E})}_{\ell m}
is the amplitude of the tidal
E
field, and similarly for the gravito-magnetic sector
354
.

We start from the tidal action (
2.21
), where we express the
E
component of the Weyl tensor in terms of the canonically-normalized graviton field
h_{\mu\nu}
in the static limit as
E_{ij}=-\partial_{i}\partial_{j}h_{00}/M_{\mathrm{Pl}}
. We can then extract the Feynman rule for the Love number vertex (at fixed
\ell
):

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}=2i{c^{(\mathrm{E})}_{\ell}}2\pi\delta(k^{0})\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\vec{q}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\,q_{\langle i_{1}}\cdots q_{i_{\ell-2}}\bar{E}(\vec{q})_{i_{\ell-1}i_{\ell}\rangle}k^{i_{1}}\cdots k^{i_{\ell-2}}\mathfrak{E}^{(0)i_{\ell-1}i_{\ell}}_{\mu\nu}(k)\,,

(2.119)

where we have introduced the functional derivative
\mathfrak{E}^{(0)ij}_{\mu\nu}\equiv\delta E^{ij}/\delta h^{\mu\nu}
, denoted the electric components of the Weyl tensor computed on the tidal solution (
2.118
) by
\bar{E}_{ij}
, and represented the coupling
{c^{(\mathrm{E})}_{\ell}}
with a black square. We are following here the conventions of Refs.
557
,
354
,
558
for the Feynman diagrams.
Note that the overall factor of 2 in
eq.

2.119
arises from the mixed term in
H
and
h
in the expansion of the action at quadratic order in the metric fluctuation, while the
2\pi\delta(k^{0})
factor results from the worldline time integral. Note also that, while the momentum
\vec{k}
is taken to be outgoing, the momentum
\vec{q}
is ingoing in the vertex; in particular, this implies an extra
(-1)^{\ell}
factor, which canceled against the
i^{2\ell-4}
from the momentum-space Fourier transform.
From
eq.

2.119
, the linear response is readily computed as

2M_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{-1}\langle h_{\mu\nu}(k)\rangle=-8\pi\frac{{c^{(\mathrm{E})}_{\ell}}}{M_{\mathrm{Pl}}}\delta(k^{0})\frac{k^{i_{1}}\cdots k^{i_{\ell-2}}}{\vec{k}^{2}}\mathfrak{E}^{(0)i_{\ell-1}i_{\ell}}_{\rho\sigma}(k)P^{\rho\sigma}{}_{\mu\nu}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\vec{q}}{(2\pi)^{3}}q_{\langle i_{1}}\cdots q_{i_{\ell-2}}\bar{E}(\vec{q})_{i_{\ell-1}i_{\ell}\rangle}\,,

(2.120)

where we used the expression (
2.104
) for the de Donder propagator.
Using
eq.

2.118
and the definition of the Weyl tensor (
2.13
), we find

\bar{E}(\vec{q})_{ij}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\ell,m}\mathcal{E}^{({\rm E})}_{\ell m}q_{i}q_{j}i^{\ell}{\cal Y}_{\ell m}^{i_{1}\cdots i_{\ell}}\frac{\partial}{\partial q^{i_{1}}}\cdots\frac{\partial}{\partial q^{i_{\ell}}}(2\pi)^{3}\delta{}^{(3)}(\vec{q})\,.

(2.121)

Transforming to real space,
eq.

2.120
becomes

2M_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{-1}\langle h_{\mu\nu}(x)\rangle=-2\frac{{c^{(\mathrm{E})}_{\ell}}}{M_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{2}}\sum_{m}\mathcal{E}^{({\rm E})}_{\ell m}\ell!{\cal Y}_{\ell m}^{i_{1}\cdots i_{\ell}}P^{00}{}_{\mu\nu}(-i)^{\ell}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\vec{k}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{k_{\langle i_{1}}\cdots k_{i_{\ell}\rangle}}{\vec{k}^{2}}e^{i\vec{k}\cdot\vec{x}}\,.

(2.122)

Finally, using the identity

(-i)^{\ell}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}\vec{k}}{(2\pi)^{d}}\frac{k^{\langle i_{1}}\cdots k^{i_{\ell}\rangle}}{\vec{k}^{2}}e^{i\vec{k}\cdot\vec{x}}=(-1)^{\ell}\frac{\Gamma(\frac{d}{2}-1)\Gamma(2-\frac{d}{2})}{2^{2-d-\ell}(4\pi)^{d/2}\Gamma(2-\ell-\frac{d}{2})}\frac{x^{\langle i_{1}}\cdots x^{i_{\ell}\rangle}}{(r^{2})^{\ell+d/2-1}}\,,

(2.123)

we can derive the linear response field
\langle h_{\mu\nu}(x)\rangle
for generic
\ell
. For instance, for the
t
–
t
component we find

\displaystyle 2M_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{-1}\langle h_{00}(x)\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{{c^{(\mathrm{E})}_{\ell}}}{M_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{2}}\sum_{m}\mathcal{E}^{({\rm E})}_{\ell m}\frac{\ell\,\Gamma(2\ell)}{2^{\ell+1}\pi}{\cal Y}_{\ell m}^{i_{1}\cdots i_{\ell}}\frac{x^{\langle i_{1}}\cdots x^{i_{\ell}\rangle}}{r^{2\ell+1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{{c^{(\mathrm{E})}_{\ell}}}{M_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{2}}\sum_{m}\mathcal{E}^{({\rm E})}_{\ell m}\frac{\ell\,\Gamma(2\ell)}{2^{\ell+1}\pi}\frac{Y_{\ell m}}{r^{\ell+1}},

(2.124)

where in the second equality we used
eq.

2.54
.
Note that
h_{00}
does not transform under a time-independent gauge transformation (in Minkowski spacetime). It is therefore a genuinely gauge-invariant quantity, which we can use to match directly to a given UV solution. For later convenience, we use
eq.

2.124
to evaluate the Weyl
E
field, which remains gauge-invariant beyond the static limit at linear order in perturbation theory. Using the definition
H_{00}(x)=r^{\ell}\mathcal{E}^{({\rm E})}_{\ell m}Y_{\ell m}
and summing over all multipoles, we obtain

E_{rr}(x)=\bar{E}_{rr}(x)+\langle E_{rr}(x)\rangle=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\ell,m}\ell(\ell-1)\mathcal{E}^{({\rm E})}_{\ell m}\left(r^{\ell-2}+\frac{{c^{(\mathrm{E})}_{\ell}}}{M_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{2}}\frac{(\ell+2)(\ell+1)\Gamma(2\ell)}{2^{\ell+1}(\ell-1)\pi}r^{-\ell-3}\right)Y_{\ell m}\,.

(2.125)

The expression (
2.125
) can be compared with the
r
–
r
component of the
E
-field obtained from the solution (
2.1a
). The result of this matching is

\bar{c}^{(\mathrm{E})}_{\ell}=k^{(\mathrm{E})}_{\ell},

(2.126)

with
\bar{c}^{(\mathrm{E})}_{\ell}
defined in
eq.

2.24
, thereby identifying the coefficients
k^{(\mathrm{E})}_{\ell}
— computed from a full-theory calculation (see
part

III
) --- with the EFT Love number couplings.
47
47

47

We stress that this is only a “tree-level” comparison. A complete matching requires accounting for gravitational nonlinearities, as we discuss in
section

2.3.4
.

Solving the equations in the presence of a tidal source

The same result can be obtained from directly solving the static equations perturbatively in the metric in the presence of a tidal field source. For simplicity, we will again focus on the gravito-electric sector (see, e.g., Refs.
389
,
340
,
301
for more details).

Once again, we start from the EFT action (
2.9
).
In what follows, we will perturb around flat space and denote the metric fluctuation by
h_{\mu\nu}
, i.e.,
g_{\mu\nu}=\eta_{\mu\nu}+2h_{\mu\nu}/M_{\mathrm{Pl}}
. Unlike in the previous section, it will be convenient to include the tidal field in
h
; we will separate tidal and response fields later.
The quadratic Einstein–Hilbert action can be read off from
eq.

2.96
, with covariant derivatives replaced by derivatives on a flat background in Cartesian coordinates, so that
(\bar{g},\bar{\nabla})\to(\eta,\partial)
.
The field
h_{\mu\nu}
is taken to satisfy the de Donder gauge (
2.100
). (Unlike the diagrammatic approach, we assume here that the tidal and response fields satisfy the same gauge condition.)
We can also decompose the metric into its space and time components using the particle’s four-velocity and the associated projector, so the de Donder gauge is
48
48

48

Because we are working in Cartesian coordinates we do not need to distinguish between upper and lower spatial indices.

\partial_{i}h_{0i}=0,\quad\partial_{j}h_{ij}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\partial_{i}h_{jj}-\partial_{i}h_{00}\right),

(2.127)

and the Einstein–Hilbert action is

\displaystyle S_{\mathrm{EH}}

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\left(-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\alpha}h_{\mu\nu}\partial^{\alpha}h^{\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{4}(\partial h)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\left(-\frac{1}{4}(\partial_{i}h_{00})^{2}-(\partial_{i}h_{0j})^{2}-\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{i}h_{jk})^{2}+\frac{1}{4}(\partial_{i}h_{jj})^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{i}h_{00}\partial_{i}h_{jj}+\dots\right),

(2.128)

where the ellipsis denotes terms containing time derivatives acting on the fields, which will not contribute in what follows since we restrict the analysis to the static response limit.
It useful to further decompose
h_{\mu\nu}
as

h_{00}=\phi,\quad h_{0i}=A_{i},\quad h_{ij}=\bar{h}_{ij}+\phi\delta_{ij},

(2.129)

where the fields
(\phi,A,\bar{h}_{ij})
do not depend on time. The de Donder gauge condition then translates to

\partial_{i}A_{i}=0,\qquad\partial_{j}\bar{h}_{ij}=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{i}\bar{h}.

(2.130)

The Einstein–Hilbert Lagrangian density, defined from the action by
S_{\mathrm{EH}}=\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{EH}}
, is conveniently computed using the double-
\epsilon
expression,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{EH}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\mu\alpha\rho\gamma}\epsilon^{\nu\beta\sigma}{}_{\gamma}\partial_{\rho}h_{\mu\nu}\partial_{\sigma}h_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\mu\alpha i\gamma}\epsilon^{\nu\beta j}{}_{\gamma}\partial_{i}h_{\mu\nu}\partial_{j}h_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{ikm}\epsilon^{jln}\partial_{i}h_{kl}\partial_{j}h_{mn}+\epsilon^{ikm}\epsilon^{jl}{}_{m}\left(\partial_{i}A_{k}\partial_{j}A_{l}-\partial_{i}\phi\partial_{j}h_{kl}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{ikm}\epsilon^{jln}\partial_{i}\bar{h}_{kl}\partial_{j}\bar{h}_{mn}-(\partial\phi)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}F_{ij}F^{ij},

(2.131)

where
F_{ij}=\partial_{i}A_{j}-\partial_{j}A_{i}
, and where we have integrated by parts in going to the third line.
Our convention is
\epsilon_{0{ij}k}=\epsilon_{{ij}k}
. We can now see why we work with
\bar{h}_{ij}
rather than
h_{ij}
: we have simply performed a field redefinition to demix
\phi
and
h_{ij}
.

The Riemann tensor to linear order in
h_{\mu\nu}
is (recalling that
h_{\mu\nu}
is related to the metric perturbation
\delta g_{\mu\nu}
by
M_{\mathrm{Pl}}\delta g_{\mu\nu}=2h_{\mu\nu}
)

\displaystyle M_{\mathrm{Pl}}R_{{\mu\nu}{\alpha\beta}}

\displaystyle=-4\partial_{[\mu|}\partial_{[\alpha}h_{\beta]|\nu]}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\beta}h_{\nu\alpha}+\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\alpha}h_{\mu\beta}-\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\beta}h_{\mu\alpha}-\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\alpha}h_{\nu\beta}.

(2.132)

On shell (i.e., when the linearized Einstein equations hold) the Riemann and Weyl tensors coincide, so it is straightforward to compute the electric and magnetic fields,

\displaystyle M_{\mathrm{Pl}}E_{ij}

\displaystyle=M_{\mathrm{Pl}}R_{0i0j}

\displaystyle=-\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\phi,

(2.133a)

\displaystyle M_{\mathrm{Pl}}B_{ij}

\displaystyle=M_{\mathrm{Pl}}\tilde{R}_{0i0j}=\frac{M_{\mathrm{Pl}}}{2}\epsilon_{0ikl}R^{kl}{}_{0j}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{ikl}\partial_{j}F^{kl}.

(2.133b)

Thus, the EFT action for
\phi
, including the higher-order terms (
2.21
) (
E
sector only), is

S_{\mathrm{EH}}^{\phi}+S_{\mathrm{ho}}^{\phi}=-\int\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,(\partial\phi)^{2}+\int\mathrm{d}\tau\sum_{\ell=2}^{\infty}\frac{{c^{(\mathrm{E})}_{\ell}}}{M_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{2}}\partial_{\langle i_{1}}\cdots\partial_{i_{\ell}\rangle}\phi\partial^{\langle i_{1}}\cdots\partial^{i_{\ell}\rangle}\phi

(2.134)

Let us now compute the response induced by an external gravito-electric tidal field. For that, it is enough to focus on the
\phi
action (
2.134
).
We can split

\phi=\phi^{(0)}+\varepsilon\phi^{(1)}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2})

(2.135)

where
\varepsilon
is a formal expansion parameter (which we will set to one in final results).
\phi^{(0)}
represents the tidal field while
\phi^{(1)}
corresponds to the induced response solution, i.e., we can identify:

\phi^{(0)}=\phi^{\mathrm{tidal}},\quad\phi^{(1)}=\phi^{\mathrm{resp}}.

(2.136)

By definition, the tidal field satisfies the bulk equations of motion (i.e., those in the absence of the point-particle interaction terms
S_{\mathrm{ho}}
), which in de Donder gauge and in the static limit reduce simply to
\nabla^{2}\phi=0
.
The corresponding tidal solution, satisfying regular boundary conditions at the location of the point particle, is

\displaystyle\phi^{\mathrm{tidal}}

\displaystyle=\frac{M_{\mathrm{Pl}}}{2}\sum_{\ell=2}^{\infty}\sum_{m=-\ell}^{\ell}\mathcal{E}^{({\rm E})}_{\ell m}r^{\ell}Y_{\ell m}(\theta,\varphi)

\displaystyle=\frac{M_{\mathrm{Pl}}}{2}\sum_{\ell=2}^{\infty}\sum_{m=-\ell}^{\ell}\mathcal{E}^{({\rm E})}_{\ell m}{\mathcal{Y}}^{\ell m}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{\ell}}x^{i_{1}}\cdots x^{i_{\ell}},

(2.137)

where in the second line we have used
eq.

2.54
. Using the identity

\partial_{i_{1}\cdots i_{\ell}}(x^{j_{1}}\cdots x^{j_{\ell}})=\ell!\delta^{(j_{1}}_{i_{1}}\cdots\delta^{j_{\ell})}_{i_{\ell}}
, we further have

\partial_{\langle i_{1}}\cdots\partial_{i_{\ell}\rangle}\phi^{\mathrm{tidal}}=\frac{M_{\mathrm{Pl}}}{2}\sum_{\ell m}\mathcal{E}^{({\rm E})}_{\ell m}\ell!{\mathcal{Y}}^{\ell m}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{\ell}}.

(2.138)

Focusing on a single multipole, the equation for the response
\phi^{\mathrm{resp}}
obtained from the action (
2.134
) at linear order in
\varepsilon
is

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}\phi^{\mathrm{resp}}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\ell+1}\frac{{c^{(\mathrm{E})}_{\ell}}}{M_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{2}}\int\mathrm{d}\tau\,\partial^{i_{1}}\cdots\partial^{i_{\ell}}\left(\delta^{(4)}(x-{X}(\tau))\partial_{\langle i_{1}}\cdots\partial_{i_{\ell}\rangle}\phi^{\mathrm{tidal}}\right)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\ell+1}\frac{{c^{(\mathrm{E})}_{\ell}}}{2M_{\mathrm{Pl}}}\mathcal{E}^{({\rm E})}_{\ell m}\ell!{\mathcal{Y}}^{\ell m}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{\ell}}\int\mathrm{d}\tau\,\partial^{i_{1}}\cdots\partial^{i_{\ell}}\delta^{(4)}(x-{X}(\tau))

\displaystyle\equiv J_{\mathrm{eff}}(x).

(2.139)

Choosing the rest frame of the object as our coordinate system, with the object located at the origin, we can write
{X}^{\mu}(\tau)=(\tau,0,0,0)
.
Then we can integrate over the worldine in
J_{\mathrm{eff}}
,

J_{\mathrm{eff}}=(-1)^{\ell+1}\frac{{c^{(\mathrm{E})}_{\ell}}}{2M_{\mathrm{Pl}}}\mathcal{E}^{({\rm E})}_{\ell m}\ell!{\mathcal{Y}}_{\ell m}^{i_{1}\cdots i_{\ell}}\partial_{i_{1}}\cdots\partial_{i_{\ell}}\delta^{(3)}(\vec{x}).

(2.140)

The solution for
\phi^{\mathrm{resp}}
can be obtained by convolving the effective source
J_{\mathrm{eff}}
with the Green’s function,

\phi^{\mathrm{resp}}(\vec{x})=[G\ast J_{\mathrm{eff}}](\vec{x})\equiv\int\mathrm{d}^{3}\vec{x}^{\prime}G(\vec{x}-\vec{x}^{\prime})J_{\mathrm{eff}}(\vec{x}^{\prime}),

(2.141)

where the Green’s function for the three-dimensional Euclidean Laplace operator is

G(\vec{x}-\vec{x}^{\prime})=-\frac{1}{4\pi|\vec{x}-\vec{x}^{\prime}|}.

(2.142)

To perform this integral it is especially convenient to go to Fourier space, where convolution becomes multiplication,

\phi^{\mathrm{resp}}(\vec{p})=G(\vec{p})J_{\mathrm{eff}}(\vec{p}).

(2.143)

The Fourier transform of the Green’s function is simply

G(\vec{p})=-\frac{1}{\vec{p}^{2}},

(2.144)

and it is similarly straightforward to calculate the momentum-space source function,

\displaystyle J_{\mathrm{eff}}(\vec{p})

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}^{3}\vec{x}\,e^{-i\vec{p}\cdot\vec{x}}J_{\mathrm{eff}}(\vec{x})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\ell+1}\frac{{c^{(\mathrm{E})}_{\ell}}}{2M_{\mathrm{Pl}}}\mathcal{E}^{({\rm E})}_{\ell m}\ell!{\mathcal{Y}}_{\ell m}^{i_{1}\cdots i_{\ell}}\int\mathrm{d}^{3}\vec{x}\,e^{-i\vec{p}\cdot\vec{x}}\partial_{i_{1}}\cdots\partial_{i_{\ell}}\delta^{(3)}(\vec{x})

\displaystyle=-\frac{{c^{(\mathrm{E})}_{\ell}}}{2M_{\mathrm{Pl}}}\mathcal{E}^{({\rm E})}_{\ell m}\ell!{\mathcal{Y}}_{\ell m}^{i_{1}\cdots i_{\ell}}\int\mathrm{d}^{3}\vec{x}\,\delta^{(3)}(\vec{x})\partial_{i_{1}}\cdots\partial_{i_{\ell}}e^{-i\vec{p}\cdot\vec{x}}

\displaystyle=-(-i)^{\ell}\frac{{c^{(\mathrm{E})}_{\ell}}}{2M_{\mathrm{Pl}}}\mathcal{E}^{({\rm E})}_{\ell m}\ell!{\mathcal{Y}}_{\ell m}^{i_{1}\cdots i_{\ell}}p_{i_{1}}\cdots p_{i_{\ell}}

(2.145)

and therefore the Fourier transform of
\phi^{\mathrm{resp}}
,

\displaystyle\phi^{\mathrm{resp}}(\vec{p})

\displaystyle=(-i)^{\ell}\frac{{c^{(\mathrm{E})}_{\ell}}}{2M_{\mathrm{Pl}}}\mathcal{E}^{({\rm E})}_{\ell m}\ell!{\mathcal{Y}}_{\ell m}^{i_{1}\cdots i_{\ell}}\frac{p_{i_{1}}\cdots p_{i_{\ell}}}{\vec{p}^{2}}.

(2.146)

Finally we go back to real space,

\displaystyle\phi^{\mathrm{resp}}(\vec{x})

\displaystyle=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\vec{p}}{(2\pi)^{3}}e^{i\vec{p}\cdot\vec{x}}\phi^{\mathrm{resp}}(\vec{p})

\displaystyle=(-i)^{\ell}\frac{{c^{(\mathrm{E})}_{\ell}}}{2M_{\mathrm{Pl}}}\mathcal{E}^{({\rm E})}_{\ell m}\ell!{\mathcal{Y}}_{\ell m}^{i_{1}\cdots i_{\ell}}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\vec{p}}{(2\pi)^{3}}e^{i\vec{p}\cdot\vec{x}}\frac{p_{i_{1}}\cdots p_{i_{\ell}}}{\vec{p}^{2}}.

(2.147)

To evaluate the momentum integral we use
eq.

2.123
:

\phi^{\mathrm{resp}}(\vec{x})=\frac{{c^{(\mathrm{E})}_{\ell}}}{M_{\mathrm{Pl}}}\mathcal{E}^{({\rm E})}_{\ell m}\frac{\Gamma(2\ell+1)}{2^{\ell+3}\pi}{\mathcal{Y}}_{\ell m}^{i_{1}\cdots i_{\ell}}\frac{x^{i_{1}}\cdots x^{i_{\ell}}}{r^{2\ell+1}}\,.

(2.148)

Putting everything together, we have

\phi=\frac{M_{\mathrm{Pl}}}{2}\sum_{\ell=2}^{\infty}\sum_{m=-\ell}^{\ell}\mathcal{E}^{({\rm E})}_{\ell m}\left(r^{\ell}+\frac{{c^{(\mathrm{E})}_{\ell}}}{M_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{2}}\frac{\Gamma(2\ell+1)}{2^{\ell+2}\pi}r^{-\ell-1}\right)Y_{\ell m}(\theta,\varphi).

(2.149)

Plugging into the definition of the Weyl tensor we find

\begin{split}E_{rr}&=-\frac{1}{M_{\mathrm{Pl}}}\partial_{r}^{2}\phi\\
&=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\ell=2}^{\infty}\sum_{m=-\ell}^{\ell}\ell(\ell-1)\mathcal{E}^{({\rm E})}_{\ell m}\left(r^{\ell-2}+\frac{{c^{(\mathrm{E})}_{\ell}}}{M_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{2}}\frac{(\ell+2)(\ell+1)\Gamma(2\ell)}{2^{\ell+1}(\ell-1)\pi}r^{-\ell-3}\right)Y_{\ell m}(\theta,\varphi),\end{split}

(2.150)

which recovers
eq.

2.125
.

Tidal response from in-in path integral

The calculation of the tidal response, as shown above using either a diagrammatic approach or a direct solution of the equations of motion, must be modified in the presence of dissipative phenomena. As discussed in
section

2.2.3
, the appropriate framework for accounting for dissipation is the in-in (Schwinger–Keldysh) formalism. In what follows, we streamline this calculation by showing how to compute the tidal response of a point particle to linear order in the frequency. For simplicity, we again focus on the parity-even sector of the perturbations.

From the in-in effective action (
2.49
), we aim to compute the one-point function of the electric component
E_{+ij}
of the Weyl tensor, in the presence of a background field
\bar{E}_{ij}
:

\langle E_{+ij}(t,\vec{x})\rangle_{\text{in-in}}=\int\mathcal{D}h_{+}\mathcal{D}h_{-}E_{+\,ij}(t,\vec{x})e^{i\Gamma^{\text{in-in}}_{\text{int}}[h_{\pm},X_{\pm}]}.

(2.151)

We compute the expectation value
of
E_{+ij}
because this is the field that has a classical interpretation. For the external
classical source we fix
h^{+}_{1}=h^{+}_{2}\equiv h^{+}
(equivalently,
E^{+}_{1}=E^{+}_{2}\equiv E^{+}
for the linearized Weyl tensor).
Using the explicit form (
2.49
), we obtain the following expression for
the one-point function
273
,
572
,
268
,
158
,
382
(see Ref.
158
for a detailed derivation):

\langle E_{+ij}(t,\vec{x})\rangle_{\text{in-in}}=i\sum_{\ell=2}^{\infty}\int\mathrm{d}\tau_{1}\mathrm{d}\tau_{2}\,K^{(\mathrm{E})}_{\ell}(\tau_{2}-\tau_{1})\langle E_{+ij}(t,\vec{x})E_{-\,A_{\ell}}\left(\tau_{2}\right)\rangle\bar{E}_{+}^{A_{\ell}}\left(\tau_{1}\right)\,,

(2.152)

where we used the multi-index notation
A_{\ell}\equiv i_{1}\cdots i_{\ell}
(see
eq.

2.22
), and where
K^{(\mathrm{E})}_{\ell}
is defined by
K^{(\mathrm{E})}_{\ell}(\tau)\delta^{\ell}_{\ell^{\prime}}\delta^{\langle i_{1}}_{\langle j_{1}}\cdots\delta^{i_{\ell}\rangle}_{j_{\ell}\rangle}\equiv{K^{(\mathrm{E})}_{+-}(\tau)^{i_{1}\cdots i_{\ell}}}_{j_{1}\cdots j_{\ell^{\prime}}}

and is related to the retarded Green’s function of the
Q
operators via
eq.

2.50
.

The response kernel can then be expanded in Fourier space similarly to
eq.

2.52
,

K^{(\mathrm{E})}_{\ell}(\tau_{2}-\tau_{1})=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}{\omega}}{2\pi}e^{-i{\omega}(\tau_{2}-\tau_{1})}K^{(\mathrm{E})}_{\ell}(\omega)=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}{\omega}}{2\pi}e^{-i{\omega}(\tau_{2}-\tau_{1})}\left(c_{0,\ell}+i{\omega}c_{1,\ell}+\cdots\right),

(2.153)

where
c_{0,\ell}
are related to the static Love numbers,
c_{1,\ell}
capture the linear-in-frequency dissipative response of the object, and so on. In the following, we will systematically neglect terms of order
\omega^{2}
and higher. See Refs.
158
,
382
for a calculation at order
\omega^{2}
.
From the definition of the Weyl tensor, we have on shell

\displaystyle E_{ij}

\displaystyle=C_{0i0j}=\frac{2}{{M_{\mathrm{Pl}}}}\left[\partial_{i}\partial_{[0}h_{j]0}-\partial_{0}\partial_{[0}h_{j]i}\right]={\frac{1}{M_{\mathrm{Pl}}}}\left[-\partial_{i}\partial_{j}h_{00}+2\partial_{0}\partial_{(i}h_{j)0}+\mathcal{O}(\omega^{2})\right],

(2.154)

\displaystyle E_{A_{\ell}}

\displaystyle={\frac{1}{M_{\mathrm{Pl}}}}\left[-\partial_{A_{\ell}}h_{00}+2\partial_{0}\partial_{\langle A_{\ell-1}}h_{i_{\ell}\rangle 0}+\mathcal{O}(\omega^{2})\right].

We next fix the de Donder gauge and expand the correlator
\langle E^{+}_{ij}(t,\vec{x})E^{-}_{A_{\ell}}\left(\tau_{2}\right)\rangle\bar{E}^{+\,A_{\ell}}\left(\tau_{1}\right)
in
eq.

2.152
up to order
\mathcal{O}(\omega)
. Using the gauge condition
\partial^{\mu}(h_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\eta_{\mu\nu}h)=0
together with the equations of motion
\square h_{\mu\nu}=0
, we note that subtracting traces in the definitions of
E^{-}_{A_{\ell}}
and
\bar{E}^{+\,A_{\ell}}
yields contributions of order
\mathcal{O}(\omega^{2})
to the product (
2.152
). Consequently, in the following we may replace
\langle{\cdots}\rangle
by
({\cdots})
.
Performing the contraction of the Weyl
E
operators then gives

E_{A_{\ell}}\bar{E}^{A_{\ell}}={\frac{1}{M_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{2}}}\left[\partial_{A_{\ell}}h_{00}\partial^{A_{\ell}}\bar{h}^{00}-2\partial_{A_{\ell}}h_{00}\partial^{0}\partial^{A_{\ell-1}}\bar{h}^{i_{\ell}0}-2\partial_{0}\partial_{A_{\ell-1}}h_{i_{\ell}0}\partial^{A_{\ell}}\bar{h}^{00}+\mathcal{O}(\omega^{2})\right].

(2.155)

Putting this together, one obtains

\langle E_{+ij}(t,\vec{x})E_{-A_{\ell}}\left(\tau_{2}\right)\rangle\bar{E}_{+}^{A_{\ell}}\left(\tau_{1}\right)=-{\frac{1}{M_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{3}}}\langle\partial_{i}\partial_{j}h_{+00}\partial_{A_{\ell}}h_{-00}\rangle\left(\partial^{A_{\ell}}\bar{h}_{+}^{00}-2\partial^{0}\partial^{A_{\ell-1}}\bar{h}_{+}^{i_{\ell}0}\right)+\mathcal{O}(\omega^{2}),

(2.156)

where we dropped terms that are either
\mathcal{\mathcal{}}{O}(\omega^{2})
or proportional to
P_{0i00}=0
, with
P_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}
defined as in
eq.

2.104
. We emphasize that
h_{\mu\nu}
and
\bar{h}_{\mu\nu}
correspond, respectively, to the response and tidal fields in the de Donder gauge. It is also useful to note that the combination in parentheses is

\partial_{A_{\ell}}\bar{h}_{00}-2\partial_{0}\partial_{A_{\ell-1}}\bar{h}_{i_{\ell}0}=\partial_{A_{\ell}}\bar{h}_{00}^{\text{RW}}+\mathcal{O}(\omega^{2}),

(2.157)

where
\bar{h}_{00}^{\text{RW}}
is the
t
–
t
component of the tidal field metric perturbation in Regge–Wheeler gauge (see Ref.
158
for a derivation), which we can express as
525

\displaystyle\bar{h}_{00}^{\text{RW}}(t,\vec{x})

\displaystyle=\frac{M_{\mathrm{Pl}}}{2}e^{-i\omega t}\sum_{\ell,m}\mathcal{E}^{(\mathrm{E})}_{\ell m}Y_{\ell}^{m}r^{\ell}+\mathcal{O}(\omega^{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{M_{\mathrm{Pl}}}{2}e^{-i\omega t}\sum_{\ell}\mathcal{E}^{(\mathrm{E})}_{j_{1}\cdots j_{\ell}}x^{j_{1}}\cdots x^{j_{\ell}}+\mathcal{O}(\omega^{2}),

(2.158)

where
\mathcal{E}^{(\mathrm{E})}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{\ell}}
is a traceless symmetric tensor.
Substituting this into
eq.

2.152
for the one-point function and employing the expression for the instantaneous graviton propagator in the de Donder gauge,

\langle h_{+\mu\nu}(t,\vec{x})h_{-\rho\sigma}(\tau_{1},\vec{0})\rangle=i\delta(t-\tau_{1})P_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\vec{p}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{e^{i\vec{p}\cdot\vec{x}}}{\vec{p}^{2}},

(2.159)

we obtain

\langle E_{+ij}(t,\vec{x})\rangle_{\text{in-in}}=-\frac{e^{-i\omega t}}{4M_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{2}}\sum_{\ell=2}^{\infty}\ell!(-i)^{\ell}K^{(\mathrm{E})}_{\ell}(\omega)\mathcal{E}^{(\mathrm{E})}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{\ell}}\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\vec{p}}{(2\pi)^{3}}e^{i\vec{p}\cdot\vec{x}}\frac{p^{i_{1}}\cdots p^{i_{\ell}}}{\vec{p}^{2}},

(2.160)

which, in position space, reads

\langle E_{+ij}(t,\vec{x})\rangle_{\text{in-in}}=-\frac{e^{-i\omega t}}{2{M_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{2}}}\sum_{\ell=2}^{\infty}K^{(\mathrm{E})}_{\ell}(\omega)\frac{\Gamma\left(2\ell+1\right)}{2^{\ell+3}\pi}\mathcal{E}^{(\mathrm{E})}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{\ell}}\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\frac{x^{i_{1}}\cdots x^{i_{\ell}}}{|\vec{x}|^{2\ell+1}},

(2.161)

where we used
eq.

2.123
.

Similarly, the background Weyl tensor can be expressed as follows. Focusing on the
r
–
r
component, we have

\displaystyle\bar{E}_{rr}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{{M_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{2}}}\left(-\partial_{r}^{2}\bar{h}_{00}+2\partial_{0}\partial_{r}\bar{h}_{0r}\right)+\mathcal{O}(\omega^{2})

(2.162)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{{M_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{2}}}\partial_{r}^{2}\bar{h}_{00}^{\text{RW}}+\mathcal{O}(\omega^{2})=-\frac{1}{2}e^{-i\omega\tau}\sum_{\ell=2}^{\infty}\mathcal{E}^{(\mathrm{E})}_{\ell m}\,\ell(\ell-1)r^{\ell-2}Y_{\ell}^{m}+\mathcal{O}(\omega^{2}).

Using
\mathcal{E}^{(\mathrm{E})}_{j_{1}\cdots j_{\ell}}x^{j_{1}}\cdots x^{j_{\ell}}=\sum_{m}\mathcal{E}^{(\mathrm{E})}_{\ell m}r^{\ell}Y_{\ell}^{m}
with
r\equiv|\vec{x}|
, and noting that
\nabla_{r}\nabla_{r}=\partial_{r}\partial_{r}
in
eq.

2.161
, then adding together
eqs.

2.161
and
2.162
we obtain

\bar{E}_{rr}+\langle E_{+\,rr}\rangle_{\text{in-in}}=-\frac{1}{2}e^{-i\omega t}\sum_{\ell,m}\ell(\ell-1)\mathcal{E}^{(\mathrm{E})}_{\ell m}Y_{\ell}^{m}\left(r^{\ell-2}+\frac{K^{(\mathrm{E})}_{\ell}(\omega)}{M_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{2}}\frac{(\ell+2)(\ell+1)\Gamma(2\ell)}{2^{\ell+2}(\ell-1)\pi}r^{-\ell-3}\right)+\mathcal{O}(\omega^{2}),

(2.163)

which agrees with
eqs.

2.125
and
2.150
upon identifying
{c^{(\mathrm{E})}_{\ell}}
with
c_{0,\ell}/2
in
eq.

2.153
(the relative factor of
2
arises from comparing the in-in action (
2.49
) with the action (
2.21
) when the latter is expressed in the Keldysh basis).

2.3.3
Tidal response from scattering amplitudes

In the previous subsection, we showed how to extract the static and dynamical responses of a compact object in the worldline EFT framework from an off-shell calculation of a graviton one-point function. One potential disadvantage of off-shell methods is that the metric computed from the graviton one-point function is a coordinate-dependent quantity. As a result, as we discussed, one must either extract a gauge-invariant quantity (such as the linearized Weyl tensor) to perform the matching, or apply an additional gauge transformation to match whatever gauge is used in the full-theory calculation.

Here, we focus on a different, on-shell observable, namely a scattering amplitude. The system we have in mind consists of a probe field scattering off a compact source:

\text{worldline}+\text{particle }X\longrightarrow\text{worldline}+\text{particle }X.

The amplitude derived in the EFT can then be matched against an analogous calculation in a fully relativistic setup, providing an independent — yet equivalent — determination of the Love numbers and dissipative couplings.

We begin by introducing the definition of the scattering matrix
S
(adopting the conventions of Ref.
572
). The transition amplitude between asymptotic states is given by

{}_{\rm out}\!\langle\vec{k}^{\prime},h^{\prime}|\vec{k},h\rangle_{\rm in}\equiv\langle\vec{k}^{\prime},h^{\prime}|S|\vec{k},h\rangle,

(2.164)

where
h
denotes the helicity of the particle. The single-particle states are normalized according to

\langle\vec{k},h|\vec{k}^{\prime},h^{\prime}\rangle=2|\vec{k}|\,(2\pi)^{3}\delta^{(3)}(\vec{k}-\vec{k}^{\prime})\,\delta_{hh^{\prime}}

(2.165)

in four spacetime dimensions. For massless particles, the on-shell condition reads
\omega^{2}=\vec{k}^{2}
.
Following the standard procedure, we further decompose the scattering matrix as

S=1+iT\,.

(2.166)

It is then convenient to make time-translation invariance manifest by isolating the energy-conserving delta function and introducing the dimensionful scattering amplitude
\mathcal{M}
through

\langle\vec{k}^{\prime},h^{\prime}|iT|\vec{k},h\rangle=(2\pi)\delta(\omega^{\prime}-\omega)\,i\mathcal{M}(\omega,\vec{k}\to\vec{k}^{\prime},h\to h^{\prime}).

(2.167)

Following Ref.
572
, to facilitate comparison with fully relativistic calculations, it is convenient to work in a spherical basis. Introducing the spherical-wave states
|\omega,\ell,m,h\rangle
, characterized by frequency
\omega
, angular momentum numbers
\ell,m
, and helicity
h
, with normalization

\langle\omega,\ell,m,h|\omega^{\prime},\ell^{\prime},m^{\prime},h^{\prime}\rangle=(2\pi)\delta(\omega-\omega^{\prime})\delta_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}\delta_{mm^{\prime}}\delta_{hh^{\prime}},

(2.168)

the scattering amplitude takes the form
572

\langle\omega^{\prime},\ell^{\prime},m^{\prime},h^{\prime}|iT|\omega,\ell,m,h\rangle=(2\pi)\delta(\omega^{\prime}-\omega)\,i\mathcal{A}(\omega,\ell,m,h,\ell^{\prime},m^{\prime},h^{\prime}),

(2.169)

where the (dimensionless) scattering matrix
\mathcal{A}
is related to
\mathcal{M}
via

\mathcal{A}(\omega,\ell,m,h,\ell^{\prime},m^{\prime},h^{\prime})=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\vec{k}_{1}\mathrm{d}^{3}\vec{k}_{2}}{4(2\pi)^{6}|\vec{k}_{1}||\vec{k}_{2}|}\sum_{h_{1},h_{2}}\langle\omega,\ell^{\prime},m^{\prime},h^{\prime}|\vec{k}_{2},h_{2}\rangle\,\mathcal{M}(\omega,\vec{k}_{1}\to\vec{k}_{2},h_{1}\to h_{2})\,\langle\vec{k}_{1},h_{1}|\omega,\ell,m,h\rangle

(2.170)

with the transformation matrix given by
273

\langle\omega,\ell,m,h|\vec{k},h^{\prime}\rangle=(2\pi)^{2}\sqrt{\frac{2\ell+1}{2\pi\omega}}\,\delta(\omega-|\vec{k}|)\,\delta_{hh^{\prime}}\,D^{\ell}_{mh}(\varphi,\theta,0),

(2.171)

where
D^{\ell}_{mh}(\varphi,\theta,0)
is the Wigner-D matrix and
(\theta,\varphi)
denotes the orientation of
\vec{k}
.

In a diagonal basis, the amplitude can be related to the scattering phase shift as

i\mathcal{A}=1-\eta_{\ell m}e^{2i\delta_{\ell m}},

(2.172)

where
\delta_{\ell m}
is the elastic scattering phase shift and

1-\eta_{\ell m}^{2}
is the absorption probability.
For gravitational perturbations, the expression for the graviton polarization in the spherical basis is (see Ref.
572
for details)

\varepsilon^{h=\pm 2}_{ij}(\vec{k})=\sum_{m}\langle i,j|\ell=2,m\rangle D^{\ell=2}_{m,h=\pm 2}(\varphi,\theta,0).

(2.173)

In the worldline theory or EFT (see
eq.

2.43
), the leading-order (zero-frequency) gravitational Compton amplitude due to induced quadrupolar tides is given by

i\mathcal{M}(\vec{k}_{\rm in}\to\vec{k}_{\rm out},h\to h)\equiv\raisebox{45.0pt}{

}=i\frac{\omega^{4}}{4M_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{2}}c^{(\mathrm{E})}_{0\,ij,kl}\,\varepsilon^{kl}_{h}(\vec{k}_{\rm in})\,\varepsilon^{*\,ij}_{h}(\vec{k}_{\rm out})+\text{magnetic}+\text{h.o.~terms},

(2.174)

where
c^{(\mathrm{E})}_{0\,ij,kl}
is obtained from the expansion of the Green’s function (
2.52
). The black dot denotes the coupling to the worldline in
eq.

2.43
, the internal dashed line represents the (retarded) propagator of the composite operator
Q
discussed in
section

2.2.3
(see
eq.

2.50
), and the curly lines correspond to the external gravitons.

In the case of a rotating point particle, we can take advantage of the decomposition (
2.84
)
572
. Assuming the spin to be oriented along the
\hat{z}
-axis, we have
\hat{s}_{i}=\delta_{i3}
, and

{J_{z}}^{i}_{j}=-i{\epsilon^{i}}_{jk}\hat{s}^{k}=-i\hat{S}^{i}{}_{j},

(2.175)

(Recall that
S_{ij}\equiv\epsilon_{ijk}\,\hat{s}_{k}
, where
\hat{s}_{k}
is the unit vector pointing along the spin of the particle in its rest frame.)
The amplitude can be thus transformed from the plane-wave basis into the spherical-wave basis as shown in Ref.
572
,

i\mathcal{A}(\omega,\ell=2,m,\ell=2,m,h)=i\frac{\omega^{5}}{40M_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{2}\pi}\bigg[\Lambda^{(\mathrm{E})}_{\omega^{0},S^{0}}+\frac{1}{2}imH^{(\mathrm{E})}_{\omega^{0},S^{1}}\\
+\frac{1}{6}(4-m^{2})\left(\Lambda^{(\mathrm{E})}_{\omega^{0},S^{2}}+imH^{(\mathrm{E})}_{\omega^{0},S^{3}}-\Lambda^{(\mathrm{E})}_{\omega^{0},S^{4}}(m^{2}-1)\right)\bigg]+\text{magnetic}+\text{h.o.~terms},

(2.176)

which holds for spinning objects at leading order in the small-frequency expansion
273
,
359
,
572
. When extended to the next-to-leading order in
\omega
, a similar calculation yields
572
,
571
,
357

i\mathcal{A}(\omega,\ell=2,m,\ell=2,m,h)=-\frac{\omega^{6}}{40M_{\mathrm{Pl}}^{2}\pi}\bigg[H^{(\mathrm{E})}_{\omega^{1},S^{0}}+\frac{1}{2}im\Lambda^{(\mathrm{E})}_{\omega^{1},S^{1}}\\
+\frac{1}{6}(4-m^{2})\left(H^{(\mathrm{E})}_{\omega^{1},S^{2}}+im\Lambda^{(\mathrm{E})}_{\omega^{1},S^{3}}-H^{(\mathrm{E})}_{\omega^{1},S^{4}}(m^{2}-1)\right)\bigg]+\text{magnetic}+\text{h.o.~terms}.

(2.177)

This result has been matched to the phase shift computed in full black hole perturbation theory
570
,
572
(see also Refs.
151
,
132
,
569
). We will return to this discussion in
section

6.2
.

2.3.4
Summary: matching Love numbers in the worldline EFT

Off-shell and on-shell approaches provide complementary ways of computing low-energy observables and performing a matching that allows one to determine the Wilson coefficients, once a complete model or theory is specified. In practice, as illustrated in the example in
section

2.3.1
, performing the matching amounts to taking some EFT quantity — such as an in-in correlation function or a phase shift — and comparing it with an analogous calculation in the full theory. Schematically:

E_{ij}^{\mathrm{GR}}=\langle E_{ij}\rangle_{\text{in-in}}^{\mathrm{EFT}}[c_{i}]\,,

(2.178)

\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{GR}}=\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{EFT}}[c_{i}]\,,

(2.179)

where on the left-hand side we have the GR quantities (the
E
field and the amplitude
\mathcal{M}
), equated on the right-hand side with the corresponding EFT quantities, which depend on the effective
couplings
c_{i}
. This allows one to determine
c_{i}
as functions of the parameters in the microscopic model.

In recent years, the combination of EFT, perturbation theory, in-in formalism, and scattering amplitudes has proven
a powerful tool to perform the matching in
eqs.

2.178
and
2.179
for the
tidal response of compact objects.
As we review in
Part

III
, examples of scattering-amplitude applications include: an on-shell proof of the vanishing of static black hole Love numbers
359
; the extraction of the leading dissipation coefficients and dynamical Love numbers
570
,
572
through a near-far factorization of GR scattering amplitudes; the calculation of both static and dynamical Love numbers for a massless scalar field through gravitational Raman scattering
355
,
112
— i.e., the inelastic scattering of massless fields off compact gravitating objects — including tidal heating effects beyond leading order and their renormalization through a non-trivial anomalous dimension
355
,
274
,
356
; and the calculation of spin-1 and spin-2 Raman amplitudes
357
. Similarly, off-shell matching has enabled the determination of the tidal response of black holes and compact objects in a variety of situations, ranging from static to dynamical tides, from linear to nonlinear response, and across different types of fields, e.g.,
389
,
118
,
340
,
273
,
132
,
549
,
301
,
268
,
103
,
557
,
354
,
157
,
496
(see
Part

III
for a more complete list of references).

One advantage of the amplitude approach is that results are immediately gauge-invariant and can be readily used in a UV matching
359
,
570
,
572
,
355
,
112
,
357
. By contrast, a potential technical disadvantage is that it requires computing far-zone quantities and accounting for the recoil of the object and tail effects, in order to ensure consistency of the in-out calculation of the dynamical gravitational response
146
,
147
,
357
. On the other hand, off-shell correlators can be evaluated within a near-zone expansion (e.g.,
132
,
340
,
389
,
158
), which is often technically simpler. A possible drawback, however, is that they are not necessarily gauge-invariant, which requires either extracting a gauge-invariant quantity (such as the linearized Weyl tensor, as in
eq.

2.178
) or performing the matching in a fixed set of coordinates.
We return to these aspects in
Part

III
.

We stress that the calculations illustrated above apply to tree-level matching only. Since gravity is a nonlinear gauge theory, a precise determination of the Wilson coefficients requires accounting for gravitational nonlinearities, which in the diagrammatic language above corresponds to evaluating higher-loop worldline diagrams. This in turn requires computing UV-divergent integrals that need to be regulated.
49
49

49

The presence of infinities arising at 5PN order was argued long ago in Ref.
186
and later fully established within the point-particle EFT
276
.
When divergences are logarithmic, their regularization necessarily introduces a renormalization scale
562
. Dimensional regularization is the natural choice here, as it respects the symmetries of the underlying action. Upon renormalization, the divergences are absorbed into the matching coefficients, which consequently acquire a scale dependence governed by the renormalization group.

As we review in
Part

III
, the static Love numbers of black holes in four-dimensional general relativity not only vanish, but also exhibit no renormalization group running — a property that reinforces the well-known naturalness puzzle of black hole tides, which recently motivated a series of symmetry proposals discussed in
section

7
. At finite frequency, by contrast, the Love numbers fulfill the standard Wilsonian expectation and display a non-trivial running. Determining the dynamical response in this case requires extending the previous calculations to loop order; see Refs.
572
,
355
,
112
,
158
,
357
for examples of EFT calculations in this direction. We provide a detailed account of the relevant literature and of what remains to be done in
Part

III
.

2.4
Post-Newtonian description of tidal effects

An alternative operational perspective on the tidal Love numbers of compact objects, traceable back to Ref.
186
, was proposed in Ref.
531
. In this approach, the induced tidal deformation is defined in terms of the moments of the body when viewed as a skeletonized object moving in a
post-Newtonian
spacetime (see Ref.
66
and references therein). The idea is based on identifying three distinct regions, defined using key scales: the mass
M
and radius
R_{*}
of the compact body whose induced moments we wish to compute; the mass scale
M^{\prime}
of the external matter responsible for the tidal environment; and the separation distance
a
between the compact body and the external matter, with the assumption that
a\gg GM,R_{*}
. In this notation, the three zones are
531
(see
fig.

2
for a sketch):

1.

Body zone:
This is the immediate neighborhood of the compact object.
It includes distances
r<r_{\max}
, where
r_{\max}
is much larger
than
GM
but much smaller than
a
. Gravity is strong deep inside this zone and is treated using full general relativity, becoming weaker toward the outer boundary.

2.

Post-Newtonian zone:
This region surrounds the compact body and includes
the external matter but excludes the innermost strong-gravity region. It covers

r_{\min}<r<\lambda_{\mathrm{r}}
, where
r_{\min}
is also much larger than
M

and much smaller than
a
, and
\lambda_{\mathrm{r}}\gg a
represents the characteristic wavelength
of the emitted gravitational radiation. Gravity here is weak and is described
using a post-Newtonian approximation.

3.

Overlap zone:
The region
r_{\min}<r<r_{\max}
lies
between the two zones. Both the fully relativistic
and post-Newtonian descriptions are valid here, allowing the two approaches to be
consistently matched.

Figure 2:
A schematic illustration the physical setup described in the main text:
Black hole (left)
— tidally distorted with an oblate event horizon, photon ring glow,
and equipotential field lines showing the tidal deformation. The figure schematically illustrates
the physical setup described in the main text: a compact body on the left interacting weakly with
a distribution of external matter on the right.

Body zone
— the strong-gravity region extending from the compact body out to

r=r_{\max}
(blue dashed boundary), where gravity is strong near the body and gradually
weakens toward the outer boundary.
Post-Newtonian zone
— the weak-field region from the external source inward
to
r=r_{\min}
(purple dashed boundary), with gravity remaining weak throughout. The unknown
matter distribution is shown as a green diffuse cloud, though we remind the reader that in inspirals it is typically another compact object. The Love numbers and dissipative response coefficients are independent of the nature of the external matter.

Overlap zone
— the shaded box covering
r_{\min}<r<r_{\max}
, where both
descriptions are simultaneously valid and can be asymptotically matched.

In the body zone, the metric is required to satisfy the vacuum Einstein field equations. The tidal environment of the body is described by a set of tidal multipole moments
\mathcal{E}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{\ell}}
, which, in this zone, are treated as freely specifiable functions of time and are not fixed by the field equations.
A representative sample of the
t
-
t
component of the body-zone metric is given by
eq.

2.1a
, which we rewrite here as (suppressing some indices for simplicity)
50
50

50

We mainly follow here the notation of Ref.

. Overdots on time-dependent functions, such as

, genuinely denote time derivatives, while overdots on coefficients, such as
\dot{k}_{2}
, are purely notational, serving as a reminder that they are associated with time-derivative terms in the metric; in the latter case, the overdot does not imply that these coefficients are themselves differentiated with respect to
t
.

\displaystyle g_{tt}=

\displaystyle\,-1+\frac{2GM}{r}-\left[r^{2}(1+\cdots)+2k_{2}R_{*}^{5}\frac{1}{r^{3}}\,(1+\cdots)\right]\mathcal{E}_{ab}\Omega^{a}\Omega^{b}

\displaystyle\,-\left[\frac{2}{7}r^{4}(1+\cdots)+2p_{2}\frac{R_{*}^{8}}{GM}\frac{1}{r^{3}}(1+\cdots)+\frac{8}{7}k_{2}R_{*}^{5}\frac{1}{r}(1+\cdots)+\frac{8}{7}k_{2}^{2}R_{*}^{10}\frac{1}{r^{6}}(1+\cdots)\right]\mathcal{E}_{c\langle a}{\mathcal{E}^{c}}_{b\rangle}\Omega^{a}\Omega^{b}

\displaystyle\,-2\dot{k}_{2}GMR_{*}^{5}\frac{1}{r^{3}}\,(1+\cdots)\,\dot{\mathcal{E}}_{ab}\Omega^{a}\Omega^{b}-\left[\frac{11}{42}r^{4}(1+\cdots)+2\ddot{k}_{2}\frac{R_{*}^{8}}{GM}\frac{1}{r^{3}}(1+\cdots)+k_{2}R_{*}^{5}\frac{1}{r}(1+\cdots)\right]\ddot{\mathcal{E}}_{ab}\Omega^{a}\Omega^{b}

\displaystyle\,+\cdots

(2.180)

where
\Omega^{a}\equiv(\sin\theta\cos\varphi,\sin\theta\sin\varphi,\cos\theta)
, and
\mathcal{E}_{ab}
are time-dependent, symmetric,
and tracefree Cartesian tensors that define the multipole moments of the external tidal field. The ellipses in the parentheses indicate higher-order terms in
GM/r
, as well as possible non-analytic contributions in
r
, while the ellipses in the last line of

2.180
refer to higher-order terms in either the (spatial or temporal) derivative expansion or the field expansion. The (constant) coefficients
k_{2}
,
p_{2}
,
\dot{k}_{2}
,

, and similarly those appearing at higher orders in the multipolar expansion, are determined by the Einstein equations in the body zone for given external moments
\mathcal{E}_{ab}
,
\dot{\mathcal{E}}_{ab}
,
\ddot{\mathcal{E}}_{ab}
, and so on.
At this level, the tidal multipole moments appear in the metric as freely specifiable functions, which can be viewed as components of the external Weyl tensor, i.e., the curvature induced by the external matter, and its derivatives.

For practical use, the metric (
2.180
) should be embedded within a larger spacetime that includes the external matter, allowing the tidal moments
\mathcal{E}_{ab}(t)
to be determined over this extended domain.
The external matter could be a companion star, multiple bodies, unbound orbits, or a diffuse distribution. In a binary system, the companion may be weakly self-gravitating, allowing a post-Newtonian description, or strongly self-gravitating, requiring full general relativity.
For instance, if the system in
fig.

2
is a two-body system, the body metric could be matched to a global metric obtained from a numerical simulation of the field equations.
The advantage of the approach in Ref.
531
is that no specific scenario needs to be specified; the essential point is that the gravitational interaction with the compact body is weak and can be accurately described using post-Newtonian theory. While the post-Newtonian data fix the tidal moments in the body zone, the relativistic information specifies the multipole structure of the post-Newtonian zone.

In practice, while the solution (
2.180
) is commonly obtained in standard Schwarzschild coordinates with a suitable gauge choice — e.g., the Regge–Wheeler gauge (see
section

4
for more details on black hole perturbation theory) — the body’s equations of motion in the post-Newtonian zone are usually solved in harmonic coordinates attached to the system’s barycenter. A detailed, general implementation of this program can be found in Ref.
531
; see Refs.
525
,
66
for an introduction to post-Newtonian gravity, and Refs.
181
,
182
,
183
,
546
,
524
,
603
for previous relevant work in this context.

In the post-Newtonian zone, the metric tensor
g_{\mu\nu}
is written in standard post-Newtonian form
603

\displaystyle g_{tt}

\displaystyle=-1+2U+2(\Psi-U^{2})+\mathcal{O}(2{\text{PN}}),

(2.181)

\displaystyle g_{ti}

\displaystyle=-4U_{i}+\mathcal{O}(2{\text{PN}}),

(2.182)

\displaystyle g_{ij}

\displaystyle=(1+2U)\delta_{ij}+\mathcal{O}(2{\text{PN}}),

(2.183)

where

x^{\mu}=(t,x^{i})
,
51
51

51

We are slightly abusing notation here. Note that

centered on the post-Newtonian barycenter, which serves as the origin of an inertial reference frame.
The post-Newtonian potential
\Psi

\Psi\equiv\psi+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial^{2}X}{\partial t^{2}},

(2.184)

in terms of two auxiliary potentials
\psi

546
,
603
,

\nabla^{2}U=0,\qquad\nabla^{2}U^{i}=0,\qquad\nabla^{2}\psi=0,\qquad\nabla^{2}X=2U,

(2.185)

where
\nabla^{2}
denotes the usual Laplace operator in three-dimensional flat space. These equations are supplemented by the harmonic coordinate condition

\partial_{t}U+\partial_{i}U^{i}=0,

(2.186)

which acts as a gauge condition on the gravitational potentials.
Each of the field equations is linear, so a solution describing both a compact body and an external matter distribution can be constructed by linear superposition. The object is modeled as a post-Newtonian monopole of mass
m
, with mass dipole and quadrupole moments
531

\displaystyle Q_{i}(t)

\displaystyle=Q_{i}[{\footnotesize\text{PN}}],

(2.187)

\displaystyle Q_{ij}(t)

\displaystyle=Q_{ij}[{\footnotesize\text{N}}]+Q_{ij}[{\footnotesize\text{PN}}],

(2.188)

respectively, with the Newtonian and post-Newtonian contributions highlighted. The point mass moves along a trajectory
\vec{x}=\vec{z}(t)
in the harmonic coordinates. Its velocity is defined by
\vec{v}=\mathrm{d}\vec{z}/\mathrm{d}t
, and its acceleration by
\vec{a}=\mathrm{d}\vec{v}/\mathrm{d}t
.
Introducing the vectors
\vec{s}\equiv\vec{x}-\vec{z}(t)
and
\vec{n}\equiv\vec{s}/s

\displaystyle U

\displaystyle=\frac{GM}{s}+\frac{1}{2}Q^{ij}[{\footnotesize\text{N}}]\partial_{ij}\frac{1}{s}+U_{\mathrm{ext}},

(2.189)

\displaystyle U^{i}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{GM}{s}+\frac{1}{2}Q^{jk}[{\footnotesize\text{N}}]\partial_{jk}\frac{1}{s}\right)v^{i}-\frac{1}{2}\dot{Q}^{ij}[{\footnotesize\text{N}}]\partial_{j}\frac{1}{s}+U^{i}_{\mathrm{ext}},

(2.190)

\displaystyle\psi

\displaystyle=\frac{GM\mu}{s}-Q^{i}[{\footnotesize\text{PN}}]\partial_{i}\frac{1}{s}+\frac{1}{2}{Q}^{ij}[{\footnotesize\text{PN}}]\partial_{ij}\frac{1}{s}+\psi_{\mathrm{ext}},

(2.191)

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle=GMs+\frac{1}{2}{Q}^{ij}[{\footnotesize\text{N}}]\partial_{ij}s+X_{\mathrm{ext}},

(2.192)

consistently with the skeletonized description of the compact body. In

\mu(t)

, to be determined by matching the post-Newtonian metric with the body-zone solution. Derivatives of

can, in turn, be re-expressed in terms of
\vec{n}
using the identities

\partial_{i}s=n_{i},\qquad\partial_{i}\frac{1}{s}=-\frac{n_{i}}{s^{2}},\qquad\partial_{ij}\frac{1}{s}=\frac{3}{s^{3}}n_{\langle ij\rangle}.

(2.193)

(See Ref.
531
for more identities.)
The potentials (
2.189
)–(
2.192
) depend on several unknowns, namely the multipole moments and the trajectory
\vec{z}(t)
of the skeletonized object. These are determined by matching, which also yields the equations of motion for the position vector. Note that the potentials become singular in the limit
s\to 0
; this singularity is however only apparent, since
s=0
lies outside the post-Newtonian zone, where
s\gg GM
.

In order to determine the unknowns, one has to compare the expressions above with corresponding quantities computed in the body zone. To perform this matching, it is first necessary to ensure that the quantities being matched are expressed in the same coordinate system. It is worth recalling that, while the post-Newtonian zone solutions are obtained in harmonic coordinates in a barycentric frame, the body-zone metric is more easily determined in a frame in which the body is positioned at the origin of the coordinate system.
The next step is therefore to perform a change of coordinates that brings the compact body to the spatial origin of the new coordinate system, referred to as the “body frame”.
The details of this transformation are worked out in Refs.
525
,
546
,
524
,
522
,
531
.

After transforming the barycentric potentials (
2.189
)–(
2.192
) from the barycentric frame --- where the compact body moves in the gravitational field of the external matter --- to the body frame --- where the body is at rest at the spatial origin --- and performing the matching in the buffer zone,
52
52

52

Depending on how the general-relativistic solution for the metric was obtained in the body zone, an additional gauge transformation may be required to express everything in the same set of (e.g., harmonic) coordinates
531
.
one obtains a relation between the mass quadrupole moment
Q_{ij}
of the skeletonized object and the tidal moments
\mathcal{E}_{ij}
. All in all
531
,
546
,

Q_{ij}=-\frac{2}{3}k_{2}R_{*}^{5}\mathcal{E}_{ij}-\frac{2}{3}p_{2}\frac{R_{*}^{8}}{GM}\mathcal{E}_{k\langle i}{\mathcal{E}^{k}}_{j\rangle}-\frac{2}{3}\ddot{k}_{2}\frac{R_{*}^{8}}{GM}\frac{1}{r^{3}}\ddot{\mathcal{E}}_{ij}+\text{PN corrections}.

(2.194)

This procedure also fixes all remaining freedom. In particular, it determines the mass dipole moment
Q_{i}
of the barycentric post-Newtonian potential, the post-Newtonian correction
\mu
to the body’s mass parameter, the relations between the tidal moments and the barycentric potentials

,

, and

generated by the external matter, the equations of motion for the vector
z(t)
, and all other details of the coordinate transformation. For a complete account, including the expression for the post-Newtonian corrections in
eq.

2.194
, we refer the reader to Ref.

.

In
eq.

2.194
, the Love numbers
k_{2}
,
p_{2}
, and
\ddot{k}_{2}
are computed in full general relativity using the tools that we will introduce in
section

4
, which provide an alternative operational definition of the induced response rooted in post-Newtonian theory. As summarized above and discussed in Ref.
531
, the multipole moments are defined in the specific (harmonic) coordinate system used to describe the post-Newtonian metric. Once the boundary conditions on the metric are fully specified — in particular, regularity at the spatial origin, vanishing ADM momentum at spatial infinity, and outgoing waves at future null infinity — all remaining freedom is fixed, up to possibly a global rotation
531
. In this sense, the post-Newtonian framework provides a practical and coordinate-invariant method for extracting the induced moments (
2.194
).

Part II
Perturbations of black holes and neutron stars

Black holes are key objects of gravitational-wave astronomy and among the most fascinating subjects in theoretical physics. Their Love numbers provide a new window into their physical and mathematical properties, as well as the fundamental structure of general relativity itself. Black holes are not, of course, the only compact objects of astrophysical relevance. Gravitational-wave observations now give us direct access to neutron stars as well, providing a unique opportunity to probe their interior structure through their tidal deformation and response in inspiraling binary systems.

In the previous section, we introduced tools that enable a robust definition of tidal responses and Love numbers in a relativistic setting, including the point-particle EFT and the PN framework. To actually compute the response coefficients, one must then match the EFT or the PN metric to a full strong-field calculation performed in the UV theory.
In the case of black hole Love numbers, this is simply vacuum general relativity in four spacetime dimensions with vanishing cosmological constant — the simplest setting and the one in which the remarkable result of vanishing Love numbers was established. For more complex objects such as neutron stars, the Einstein equations are sourced by a matter stress-energy tensor, which couples fluid perturbations inside the star to the exterior metric perturbations.

To describe tidal deformations of black holes, we consider perturbations of the Schwarzschild and Kerr metrics. In this Part, after briefly reviewing black hole solutions in general relativity (
section

3
), we work within linear perturbation theory, which will suffice to calculate the leading-order Love numbers in
Part

III
. The results derived in
sections

3
and
4
describe the exterior geometry of any compact object in vacuum general relativity --- including Schwarzschild and Kerr black holes as well as the exterior of neutron stars or other compact bodies with a physical surface.
53
53

53

This is strictly true for non-spinning objects, where Birkhoff’s theorem guarantees that the exterior of a non-rotating star is described by the Schwarzschild metric. There is no analog of Birkhoff’s theorem for rotating objects, but in practice the exterior spacetime of neutron stars and other compact objects is extremely close to Kerr.
The distinction between object types enters entirely through the boundary conditions: for black holes one imposes ingoing-wave conditions at the event horizon, whereas for objects with a surface one matches to an interior solution at the stellar radius. The Teukolsky formalism
607
provides a unified framework for both cases, as we discuss in
section

4
.

We review the basics of stellar perturbation theory in
section

5
, where we derive the equations governing perturbations of a perfect fluid coupled to gravity, which are needed to describe the interior physics and to determine the boundary conditions required to compute the tidal response (cf.
section

6.6
).

General relativity is an intrinsically nonlinear theory, and a complete treatment of tidal response beyond leading order requires higher-order perturbation theory. Known results for nonlinear Love numbers, obtained within both perturbation theory and effective field theory, are discussed in
section

6.3
of
Part

III
. Extensions beyond the four-dimensional vacuum baseline — including charged black holes, higher dimensions, non-zero cosmological constant, and modifications of GR — will be covered in
section

6.4
.

3
Black hole solutions in general relativity

We begin by reviewing the “background” solutions, namely the famous Schwarzschild and Kerr spacetimes of four-dimensional general relativity. This review will necessarily be brief and focused on aspects of these geometries that are relevant for the problem at hand, such as their symmetries and the behavior of massless test particles and waves. For a more thorough introduction to black holes, see, e.g., Refs.
615
,
130
.

3.1
Schwarzschild

3.1.1
Geometry

The Schwarzschild spacetime, which describes a non-rotating black hole, was the first exact, non-trivial solution to Einstein’s field equations of general relativity to be discovered. As a consequence of Birkhoff’s theorem, it describes the exterior of all spherically-symmetric bodies. In Schwarzschild coordinates
(t,r,\theta,\varphi)
,
54
54

54

These coordinates only cover the exterior
r>r_{\mathrm{s}}
, and this is all we will need for computing tidal responses.
the Schwarzschild metric is

\mathrm{d}s^{2}=-f(r)\mathrm{d}t^{2}+\frac{1}{f(r)}\mathrm{d}r^{2}+r^{2}\mathrm{d}\Omega^{2}_{2}.

(3.1)

Here we have defined the function

f(r)=1-\frac{r_{\mathrm{s}}}{r},

(3.2)

with the
Schwarzschild radius
related to the mass
M
by

r_{\mathrm{s}}=2GM,

(3.3)

as well as the metric on the unit 2-sphere,

\mathrm{d}\Omega^{2}_{2}=\mathrm{d}\theta^{2}+\sin^{2}\theta\mathrm{d}\varphi^{2}.

(3.4)

We will also find it convenient to work with the function

\Delta(r)\equiv r(r-r_{\mathrm{s}})=r^{2}f(r).

(3.5)

At the origin,
r=0
, and the Schwarzschild radius,
r=r_{\mathrm{s}}
, this metric is singular,

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}s^{2}\overset{r\to r_{\mathrm{s}}}{\longrightarrow}0\,\mathrm{d}t^{2}+\frac{1}{0}\mathrm{d}r^{2}+r_{\mathrm{s}}^{2}\mathrm{d}\Omega_{2}^{2},

(3.6a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}s^{2}\overset{r\to 0}{\longrightarrow}\frac{1}{0}\,\mathrm{d}t^{2}+0\mathrm{d}r^{2}+r_{\mathrm{s}}^{2}\mathrm{d}\Omega_{2}^{2}.

(3.6b)

The singularity at
r_{\mathrm{s}}
is not physical, but rather a coordinate singularity, which can be removed by a suitable coordinate transformation. By contrast the singularity at
r=0
is a genuine curvature singularity, signalling a breakdown of the spacetime description.

The 2-sphere at
r=r_{\mathrm{s}}
is known as the
event horizon
. It is the point of no return: an observer starting at
r<r_{\mathrm{s}}
cannot escape the gravitational field, and reaches the central singularity within finite proper time. For this reason objects contained within
r=r_{\mathrm{s}}
are called
black holes
: past the event horizon, the “escape velocity” exceeds the speed of light. Consider for simplicity a null geodesic at fixed angle,

\mathrm{d}s^{2}=0=-f\mathrm{d}t^{2}+\frac{1}{f}\mathrm{d}r^{2}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\frac{\mathrm{d}r}{\mathrm{d}t}=\left|1-\frac{r_{\mathrm{s}}}{r}\right|,

(3.7)

which exceeds unity when
r<r_{\mathrm{s}}
.
Objects with radius
R>r_{\mathrm{s}}
are not described by the Schwarzschild metric for
r<R
, since the Einstein equation in the interior is sourced by the stress-energy tensor of the matter.

3.1.2
Symmetries

The Schwarzschild solution is highly symmetric. The metric is manifestly invariant under rotations and time translations. The isometries of a spacetime are encoded in its Killing vectors, that is, directions
\xi^{\mu}
along which the Lie derivative of the metric vanishes,

\mathcal{L}_{\xi}g_{\mu\nu}=\nabla_{(\mu}\xi_{\nu)}=0.

(3.8)

The lack of explicit
t
and
\varphi
dependence in the metric tells us that the unit vectors in those directions are Killing,

\xi_{1}=\partial_{t},\quad\xi_{2}=\partial_{\varphi},

(3.9)

where
v\equiv v^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}
, while spherical symmetry gives us two more Killing directions by rotating
\xi_{2}
, explicitly,

\xi_{3}=\cos\varphi\partial_{\theta}-\cot\theta\sin\varphi\partial_{\varphi},\quad\xi_{4}=\sin\varphi\partial_{\theta}+\cot\theta\cos\varphi\partial_{\varphi}.

(3.10)

We say that the Schwarzschild metric is
static
and
spherically symmetric
. Here a static metric means one which possesses a timelike Killing vector (
\partial_{t}g_{\mu\nu}=0
) and is invariant under
time reversal
(
g_{ti}=0
).
We note for future reference that if the latter condition is not satisfied, the spacetime is instead called
stationary
.

3.1.3
Geodesics

Linear perturbations of the metric propagate as massless waves. Before dealing with linearized general relativity, it is instructive to anticipate some of its qualitative features by investigating the behavior of massless particles and waves on the background.

Test particles follow geodesics, whose parametrized path
x^{\mu}(\affine)
satisfies the geodesic equation,

\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}x^{\mu}}{\mathrm{d}{\affine}^{2}}+\Gamma^{\mu}_{\alpha\beta}\frac{\mathrm{d}x^{\alpha}}{\mathrm{d}{\affine}}\frac{\mathrm{d}x^{\beta}}{\mathrm{d}{\affine}}=0,

(3.11)

with
\affine
an affine parameter along the path and
\Gamma^{\mu}_{\alpha\beta}
the Christoffel symbols,

\Gamma^{\mu}_{\alpha\beta}=g^{\mu\nu}\left(\partial_{(\alpha}g_{\beta)\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\nu}g_{\alpha\beta}\right).

(3.12)

A useful shortcut to avoid calculating the Christoffel symbols is to consider the point-particle Lagrangian,
55
55

55

This is not precisely the point-particle action (
2.3
) but rather (up to terms not involving
\affine
) its square. The equations of motion following from variation are the same.

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=g_{\mu\nu}\dot{x}^{\mu}\dot{x}^{\nu}

\displaystyle=-f\dot{t}^{2}+\frac{\dot{r}^{2}}{f}+r^{2}\left(\dot{\theta}^{2}+\sin^{2}\theta\dot{\varphi}^{2}\right),

(3.13)

where overdots denote derivatives with respect to
\affine
. The components of the geodesic equation (
3.11
) are the Euler-Lagrange equations with respect to each of the spacetime coordinates,

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}{\affine}}\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\dot{x}^{\mu}}-\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial x^{\mu}}=0.

(3.14)

Geodesic motion in the Schwarzschild spacetime has a special property: it is completely integrable, and therefore can be characterized fully by first-order constraints.
First we notice that geodesic motion in Schwarzschild is planar. Consider a particle placed at an arbitrary point in the spacetime, and use the spherical symmetry to rotate to a coordinate system in which the particle is located on the equator,
\theta=\pi/2
, and its instantaneous velocity in the
\theta
direction is also along the equator,
\dot{\theta}=0
. From the Euler-Lagrange equation for
\theta
,

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}{\affine}}\left(r^{2}\dot{\theta}\right)-r^{2}\sin\theta\cos\theta\dot{\varphi}^{2},

(3.15)

it is clear that we may take
\theta=\pi/2
without loss of generality. For the
t
and
\varphi
directions, the geodesic equation can be integrated, since

\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial t}=\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\varphi}=0.

(3.16)

The
t
and
\varphi
components of the geodesic equation are therefore each the first derivative of a conservation law,

\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\dot{t}}=-f\dot{t}\equiv-E,\qquad\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\dot{\varphi}}=r^{2}\dot{\varphi}\equiv h.

(3.17)

Physically,
E
is the energy per unit mass of the particle at
r=\infty
, while
h
is the angular momentum per unit mass. These conserved quantities follow directly from the existence of the Killing vectors
\partial_{t}
and
\partial_{\varphi}
, due to an avatar of Noether’s theorem: along a geodesic
x^{\mu}(\affine)
the product
\xi_{\mu}\dot{x}^{\mu}
is conserved,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}{\affine}}\left(\xi_{\mu}\dot{x}^{\mu}\right)

\displaystyle=\dot{x}^{\nu}\nabla_{\nu}\left(\xi_{\mu}\dot{x}^{\mu}\right)

\displaystyle=\dot{x}^{\mu}\dot{x}^{\nu}\nabla_{\nu}\xi_{\mu}+\xi_{\mu}\dot{x}^{\nu}\nabla_{\nu}\dot{x}^{\mu}=0.

(3.18)

The first term vanishes due to symmetry and the Killing equation (
3.8
), and the second by virtue of the geodesic equation (
3.11
), which we can write as
\dot{x}^{\nu}\nabla_{\nu}\dot{x}^{\mu}=0
.

To completely characterize the geodesics, all that remains is to solve for
r(\affine)
. Normally this would require us to solve the
r
component of the geodesic equation. However on Schwarzschild we have enough conserved quantities that geodesic motion is integrable, with
r(\affine)
being determined by the normalization of the 4-velocity,
56
56

56

This can also be seen as a conservation law, namely conservation of mass.

\displaystyle g_{\mu\nu}\dot{x}^{\mu}\dot{x}^{\nu}

\displaystyle=-f\dot{t}^{2}+\frac{\dot{r}^{2}}{f}+r^{2}\dot{\varphi}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\dot{r}^{2}-E^{2}}{f}+\frac{h^{2}}{r^{2}}=\begin{cases}1&\mathrm{spacelike}\\
0&\mathrm{lightlike}\\
-1&\mathrm{timelike}\end{cases}.

(3.19)

In particular we will be interested in perturbations of massless fields such as the gravitational or electromagnetic fields, which in the geometric optics limit propagate along lightlike (
g_{\mu\nu}\dot{x}^{\mu}\dot{x}^{\nu}=0
) geodesics. Solving for
\dot{r}
we obtain, in effect, a first integral of the
r
geodesic equation,

\dot{r}^{2}=E^{2}-\frac{f}{r^{2}}h^{2}.

(3.20)

This is precisely analogous to the conservation of energy for a particle in an effective potential,

\frac{1}{2}\dot{r}^{2}+V_{\mathrm{eff}}(r)=\frac{1}{2}E^{2},\qquad V_{\mathrm{eff}}(r)=\frac{1}{2}\frac{h^{2}f}{r^{2}}.

(3.21)

We plot
V_{\mathrm{eff}}(r)
in
fig.

3
.
The potential has no minima and a single maximum, located at the
photon sphere
,

r_{\mathrm{ps}}=\frac{3}{2}r_{\mathrm{s}}.

(3.22)

Physically this means that massless particles can only orbit a Schwarzschild black hole at the radius
r=r_{\mathrm{ps}}
, and that moreover such orbits are unstable; a photon orbiting just inside or outside the photon sphere will fall into the black hole or be scattered out to infinity, respectively.

3.1.4
Wave equation

Closely related to the problem of massless test particle motion is the
behavior of massless waves. In the geometric optics approximation
these precisely follow null geodesics, and one might expect the
full wave equation to behave similarly.
For
simplicity
57
57

57

The spin-2 wave equation, which results from linearizing Einstein’s equations around Schwarzschild, is studied in
section

4.1
. The utility of the massless scalar as a simplified model displaying the qualitative features of gravitational perturbations will be a theme throughout
Part

III
.
we consider the spin-0 wave equation, i.e., the
Klein–Gordon equation for a massless scalar
\phi
,

\Box\phi=0.

(3.23)

Again we do not need to compute Christoffel symbols, as the d’Alembertian is simply

\displaystyle 0=\Box\phi

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{-g}}\partial_{\mu}\left(\sqrt{-g}\partial^{\mu}\phi\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{r^{2}\sin\theta}\partial_{\mu}\left(r^{2}\sin\theta g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\nu}\phi\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{r^{4}}{\Delta}\partial_{t}^{2}\phi+\partial_{r}\left(\Delta\partial_{r}\phi\right)+\nabla^{2}_{S^{2}}\phi,

(3.24)

where in the last line we multiplied by
r^{2}
for convenience, and the Laplacian on the 2-sphere is

\nabla^{2}_{S^{2}}=\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\partial_{\theta}\left(\sin\theta\partial_{\theta}\right)+\frac{1}{\sin^{2}\theta}\partial_{\varphi}^{2}.

(3.25)

We solve
eq.

3.24
by assuming a separable form for the field:

\phi(x^{\mu})=e^{-i(\omega t-m\varphi)}\,\phi(r)\,S(\theta),

(3.26)

where
\phi(r)
and
S(\theta)
are functions of the radial and polar coordinates, respectively. The simple exponential dependence on
t
and
\varphi
is a direct
result of their being Killing directions, in analogy to the
conservation laws discussed above: in the case of the wave equation, the statement is that
\phi
is an eigenfunction of the Killing vectors
\partial_{t}
and
\partial_{\varphi}
. Stationarity yields a continuous
Fourier decomposition in
t
with frequency
\omega
, while
axisymmetry yields a discrete decomposition in
\varphi
with integer
azimuthal number
m
.

Plugging this ansatz into the Klein–Gordon equation and dividing out an overall factor of
\phi(x^{\mu})
we find

0=\frac{1}{\phi(r)}\left[\partial_{r}\left(\Delta\partial_{r}\phi(r)\right)+\frac{\omega^{2}r^{4}}{\Delta}\phi(r)\right]+\frac{1}{\sin\theta S(\theta)}\left[\partial_{\theta}\left(\sin\theta\partial_{\theta}S(\theta)\right)-m^{2}\csc\theta S(\theta)\right].

(3.27)

The first term depends only on
r
while the second term depends only on
\theta
, so they must both be constants,

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\left(\Delta\partial_{r}\phi\right)+\frac{\omega^{2}r^{4}}{\Delta}\phi

\displaystyle=\ell(\ell+1)\phi,

(3.28)

\displaystyle\partial_{\theta}\left(\sin\theta\partial_{\theta}S\right)-m^{2}\csc\theta S

\displaystyle=-\ell(\ell+1)\sin\theta S.

(3.29)

Here
\ell
is a constant which, as we will soon see, is required to be a non-negative integer. The
\theta
equation is the general Legendre differential equation (
A.19
) in slight disguise, as can be seen by defining
x=\cos\theta
,

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}\left[(1-x^{2})\frac{\mathrm{d}S}{\mathrm{d}x}\right]-\left[\ell(\ell+1)-\frac{m^{2}}{1-x^{2}}\right]S=0.

(3.30)

The solutions are the associated Legendre functions
484
,
10
,

S(\theta)=c_{1}P_{\ell}^{m}(\cos\theta)+c_{2}Q_{\ell}^{m}(\cos\theta).

(3.31)

The associated Legendre function of the second kind,
Q_{\ell}^{m}(x)
, diverges at the poles,
x=\pm 1
, so on physical grounds we set
c_{2}=0
. The Legendre functions of the first kind also diverge at the pole
\theta=\pi
(
x=-1
) when
\ell
is not an integer. We see that the angular eigenfunctions are nothing other than the standard spherical harmonics, as the reader might have anticipated from the spherical symmetry of the system or from the fact that
e^{im\varphi}S(\theta)
is clearly an eigenfunction of
\nabla^{2}_{S^{2}}
,

e^{im\varphi}S(\theta)\propto Y_{\ell}^{m}(\theta,\varphi)=Ne^{im\varphi}P_{\ell}^{m}(\cos\theta),

(3.32)

with
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}
,
-\ell\leq m\leq\ell
, and
N
a normalization constant whose form is given in
eq.

A.26
. For more on the spherical harmonics see
A.3
.

The remaining equation of motion is for the radial field,

\boxed{\partial_{r}\left(\Delta\partial_{r}\phi\right)+\frac{\omega^{2}r^{4}}{\Delta}\phi-\ell(\ell+1)\phi=0.}

(3.33)

The reader is invited to compare this to the Poisson equation (
1.18
) in Newtonian gravity, recalling that in the flat-space limit
\Delta=r^{2}
. To canonically normalize this equation we rescale
\phi
,

\psi\equiv r\phi,

(3.34)

in terms of which

\boxed{\partial_{{r_{\star}}}^{2}\psi+\left(\omega^{2}-\frac{ff^{\prime}}{r}-\ell(\ell+1)\frac{f}{r^{2}}\right)\psi=0.}

(3.35)

Here
{r_{\star}}
is the
tortoise coordinate
, defined by

\mathrm{d}{r_{\star}}=\frac{\mathrm{d}r}{f}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad f\partial_{r}=\partial_{{r_{\star}}}.

(3.36)

This integrates to

{r_{\star}}=r+r_{\mathrm{s}}\ln\left(\frac{r}{r_{\mathrm{s}}}-1\right),

(3.37)

with the horizon located at
{r_{\star}}=-\infty
and spatial infinity at
{r_{\star}}=+\infty
.

The Klein–Gordon equation
\Box\phi=0
on Schwarzschild therefore reduces to the 1D ODE (
3.33
) or (
3.35
).
The field
\psi(r)
obeys a Schrödinger-type equation in an effective potential,

\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}r_{\star}^{2}}\psi=(V_{\ell}(r)-\omega^{2})\psi

(3.38)

with

\displaystyle V_{\ell}(r)

\displaystyle=\frac{f}{r^{2}}\left(\ell(\ell+1)+rf^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{f}{r^{2}}\left(\ell(\ell+1)+\frac{r_{\mathrm{s}}}{r}\right).

(3.39)

The potentials
V_{\ell}(r)
are plotted for
\ell=1,2
in
fig.

3
.
The general solution to
eq.

3.33
is built by integrating over all frequencies and summing over all values of
\ell
and
m
,
