\cdot&\cdot&-h_{2}(t,r)\csc\theta\nabla_{\theta}\nabla_{\varphi}&\frac{1}{2}h_{2}(t,r)\left(\sin\theta\partial_{\theta}^{2}-\csc\theta\nabla_{\varphi}^{2}\right)\\
\cdot&\cdot&\cdot&h_{2}(t,r)\sin\theta\nabla_{\theta}\nabla_{\varphi}\end{pmatrix}Y_{\ell m}.

(5.10b)

(Note that, compared to
eq.

4.11
, the metric perturbation components are defined here with a non-canonical normalization, and the
t
–
t
and
r
–
r
components have been rescaled by different prefactors.)
The fluid four-velocity can be analogously decomposed as
385

\delta u^{\mu}=\frac{e^{\Phi/2}}{4\pi(\bar{\rho}+\bar{p})}\left[\begin{pmatrix}\delta u^{0}(t,r)\\
e^{-\Psi}R(t,r)\\
r^{-2}V(t,r)\partial_{\theta}\\
r^{-2}\csc^{2}\theta V(t,r)\partial_{\varphi}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}0\\
0\\
-r^{-2}U(t,r)\csc\theta\partial_{\varphi}\\
r^{-2}U(t,r)\sin\theta\partial_{\theta}\end{pmatrix}\right]Y_{{\ell m}},

(5.11)

where the two terms in brackets are of even and odd parity, respectively.
The normalization condition for the four-velocity,
u^{\mu}u_{\mu}=-1
, determines
\delta u^{0}
in terms of the remaining perturbations. To first order in perturbation theory, one obtains
88
88

88

See Ref.
496
for a generalization at second-order in perturbation theory.

\delta u^{0}=2\pi e^{-\Phi}(\bar{\rho}+\bar{p})H_{0}.

(5.12)

In the following, we work in Regge–Wheeler gauge
551
, defined by the conditions
h_{2}=\mathcal{H}_{0}=\mathcal{H}_{1}=\mathcal{G}=0
.

5.2.1
Linearized polar equations

Since the equations of motion for the even- and odd-parity metric components decouple to linear order in perturbation theory, they can be analyzed independently. We begin by focusing on the parity-even (polar) sector
128
,
350
,
129
,
385
,
609
,
199
.
Because the equation of motion
\mathbf{E}_{\mu\nu}
(
5.3
) is a rank-two tensor, it can be decomposed into tensor spherical harmonics using the same basis as for the metric perturbations. After projecting out the angular dependence through standard spherical-harmonic identities, and denoting by capital Latin indices
A,B,C,\ldots
the angular coordinates on the two-dimensional sphere
S^{2}
, whose line element is
\gamma_{AB}{\rm d}x^{A}{\rm d}x^{B}\equiv\mathrm{d}\Omega_{S^{2}}^{2}={\rm d}\theta^{2}+\sin^{2}\theta\,{\rm d}\varphi^{2}
, the components of
\mathbf{E}_{\mu\nu}
take the form

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{tt}=

\displaystyle\ \frac{e^{\Phi}}{2r^{2}}\big\{2\left[(r-2{\cal M})(H_{2}^{\prime}-r\,K^{\prime\prime})+\left(5{\cal M}+4\pi r^{3}\bar{\rho}-3r\right)K^{\prime}-8\pi r^{2}\delta\rho\right]

\displaystyle+\left(\ell(\ell+1)-16\pi r^{2}\bar{\rho}+2\right)H_{2}+\left(\ell(\ell+1)-2\right)K\big\}Y_{\ell m}(\theta,\varphi)\;,

(5.13)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{tr}=

\displaystyle\ \frac{1}{2r^{2}}\left\{H_{1}\ell(\ell+1)+2r\left[-i\omega(H_{2}-rK^{\prime})-\frac{i\omega\left(3{\cal M}+4\pi r^{3}\bar{p}-r\right)K}{r-2{\cal M}}+2re^{\Phi}R\right]\right\}Y_{\ell m}(\theta,\varphi)\;,

(5.14)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{rr}=

\displaystyle\ \frac{e^{-\Phi}}{2r(r-2{\cal M})}\big\{-e^{\Phi}\big[2(r-2{\cal M})H_{0}^{\prime}+2\left(1+8\pi r^{2}\bar{p}\right)H_{2}+2\left({\cal M}-4\pi r^{3}\bar{p}-r\right)K^{\prime}+16\pi r^{2}\delta p

\displaystyle-\ell(\ell+1)H_{0}+(\ell(\ell+1)-2)K\big]+2\omega\big(r^{2}\omega K-2i(r-2{\cal M})H_{1}\big)\big\}Y_{\ell m}(\theta,\varphi)\;,

(5.15)

\displaystyle\gamma^{AB}\mathbf{E}_{AB}=

\displaystyle\ \frac{e^{-\Phi}}{2}\big\{e^{\Phi}\big[\ell(\ell+1)H_{0}-\left(\ell(\ell+1)+32\pi r^{2}\bar{p}\right)H_{2}-2\left(r+{\cal M}+4\pi r^{3}(2\bar{p}-\bar{\rho})\right)H_{0}^{\prime}

\displaystyle-2\left(r-{\cal M}+4\pi r^{3}\bar{p}\right)H_{2}^{\prime}-2r(r-2{\cal M})(H_{0}^{\prime\prime}-K^{\prime\prime})+4\left(r-{\cal M}+2\pi r^{3}(\bar{p}-\bar{\rho})\right)K^{\prime}-32\pi r^{2}\delta p\big]

\displaystyle+2r\omega\left[r\omega\left(H_{2}+K\right)-2i(r-2{\cal M})H_{1}^{\prime}\right]+4i\omega H_{1}\left({\cal M}+4\pi r^{3}\bar{\rho}-r\right)\big\}Y_{\ell m}(\theta,\varphi)\;,

(5.16)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{tA}=

\displaystyle\ \frac{1}{2r^{2}}\left\{r\left[(r-2{\cal M})H_{1}^{\prime}+4\pi r^{2}(\bar{p}-\bar{\rho})H_{1}+ir\omega K+4re^{\Phi}V\right]+2{\cal M}H_{1}+ir^{2}\omega H_{2}\right\}\nabla_{A}Y_{\ell m}(\theta,\varphi)\;,

(5.17)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{rA}=

\displaystyle\ \frac{1}{2}\left[\frac{(3{\cal M}-r+4\pi r^{3}\bar{p})H_{0}-({\cal M}-r+4\pi r^{3}\bar{p})H_{2}}{r(r-2{\cal M})}+i\omega e^{-\Phi}H_{1}+H_{0}^{\prime}-K^{\prime}\right]\nabla_{A}Y_{\ell m}(\theta,\varphi)\;,

(5.18)

\displaystyle[\mathbf{E}_{AB}]_{\rm{STF}}=

\displaystyle\ \frac{1}{2}\left(H_{0}-H_{2}\right)\left(\nabla_{A}\nabla_{B}-\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{AB}\nabla_{C}\nabla^{C}\right)Y_{\ell m}(\theta,\varphi)\;,

(5.19)

where a prime denotes differentiation with respect to the radial coordinate,
\nabla_{A}
is the covariant derivative on
S^{2}
, and
[{\cdots}]_{\rm STF}
in
eq.

5.19
indicates the symmetric trace-free part. In deriving these expressions, the TOV equations (
5.4
)–(
5.6
) have been used to simplify the results.
The system consists of seven equations for eight perturbation variables:
H_{0}
,
H_{1}
,
H_{2}
,
K
,
R
,
V
,
\delta\rho
, and
\delta p
. Therefore, it can be closed upon supplementing it with an EoS.
Clearly, not all of these variables are dynamical. The step to integrate out the constraint fields and simplify the set of eqs. (
5.4
)–(
5.6
), once an EoS is fixed, can be found in, e.g., Refs.
245
,
128
,
350
,
129
,
385
.

In summary,
eq.

5.19
yields the constraint
H_{2}=H_{0}
. The equations
\mathbf{E}_{tr}=0
and
\mathbf{E}_{tA}=0
can, in general, be used to express
R
and
V
in terms of
H_{0}
,
H_{1}
,
K
, and their radial derivatives. Moreover,
\delta p
— and hence
\delta\rho
through the equation of state — can be written in terms of the same set of variables by means of
\mathbf{E}_{rr}=0
.
The remaining functions,
H_{0}
,
H_{1}
, and
K
, are then determined by solving the reduced system given by
\mathbf{E}_{tt}=0
,
\mathbf{E}_{rA}=0
, and
\gamma^{AB}\mathbf{E}_{AB}=0
.

Note that the system of eqs. (
5.13
)–(
5.19
) is exact to linear order in perturbation theory (see Refs.
519
,
496
for an extension to second order). In particular, the system is valid for arbitrary frequency
\omega
.
To investigate the tidal response in the adiabatic régime, one would like to consider the small-frequency limit of eqs. (
5.13
)–(
5.19
).
However, this limit can be subtle. If one sets
\omega=0
directly in these equations, the variables
V
,
R
,
H_{1}
appear to remain unconstrained. As discussed above, the equations
\mathbf{E}_{tr}=0
and
\mathbf{E}_{tA}=0
can be used to express
V
and
R
in terms of
H_{1}
. However, when
\omega
vanishes exactly,
H_{1}
drops out of the remaining equations. In particular, one can no longer use, for example
\mathbf{E}_{rA}=0
to solve for
H_{1}
in terms of
H_{2}
,
H_{0}
and
K
.

Although this does not pose a fundamental problem for the linear static response in the even sector — where
H_{1}
is not required explicitly to determine
H_{0}

333
— it may be problematic at second order
496
. At least formally, this issue can be resolved by working carefully in the adiabatic limit
\omega\rightarrow 0
, rather than setting
\omega=0
from the outset.
89
89

89

This subtlety can also be seen in our analysis of vacuum Schwarzschild perturbations in
section

4.1
. For finite
\omega
,
H_{1}
is determined by
eq.

4.18
, and goes to zero in the limit
\omega\to 0
. If we instead set
\omega=0
at the start,
H_{1}
drops out of the action (
4.16a
) entirely and formally has no equation of motion.

After solving the constraint equations at finite
\omega
, one finds that the frequency enters only quadratically in the remaining equations for
K
and
H_{0}
(see, e.g., Refs.
350
,
385
).
Consequently, the solutions at orders
\mathcal{O}(\omega^{0})
and
\mathcal{O}(\omega)
coincide in the small-
\omega
limit for these variables.
It then follows that, at order
\mathcal{O}(\omega)
,
\mathbf{E}_{rA}=0
reduces simply to
\omega H_{1}=0
, implying
H_{1}=\mathcal{O}(\omega)
. Likewise, from
\mathbf{E}_{tr}=0
and
\mathbf{E}_{tA}=0
one finds
V,R=\mathcal{O}(\omega)
.
Therefore, if one is interested only in the properties of the linearized perturbations at order
\mathcal{O}(\omega^{0})
it is consistent to set
V=R=H_{1}=0
. As a result, the components
\mathbf{E}_{tr}
and
\mathbf{E}_{tA}
vanish identically at this order.

At leading order in the static limit, the remaining equations simplify significantly. One can use
\mathbf{E}_{rA}=0
to solve for
K^{\prime}
, and
\gamma^{AB}\mathbf{E}_{AB}=0
to solve for
K
in terms of
H_{0}
and
H_{0}^{\prime}
.
In addition, solving
\mathbf{E}_{tt}=0
for
\delta\rho
, one finds

\delta\rho=\frac{1}{2}\frac{\bar{\rho}^{\prime}}{\bar{p}^{\prime}}(\bar{p}+\bar{\rho})H_{0}\;.

(5.20)

Finally, after introducing the sound speed

c_{s}^{2}(r)\equiv\frac{\bar{p}^{\prime}(r)}{\bar{\rho}^{\prime}(r)}\;,

(5.21)

such that
\delta p=c_{s}^{2}\delta\rho
,
90
90

90

A major simplification arises in the static limit. Because the perturbations evolve on timescales much longer than those associated with heat transfer, the fluid effectively maintains local thermodynamic equilibrium throughout the process. As a result, the perturbations are fully characterized by a single fluid degree of freedom, leading to the relation
\delta p/p^{\prime}=\delta\rho/\rho^{\prime}
, as can be checked explicitly.
one obtains a single second-order differential equation for
H_{0}

333
,

0=H_{0}^{\prime\prime}+H_{0}^{\prime}\frac{2\left[r-{\cal M}+2\pi r^{3}(\bar{p}-\bar{\rho})\right]}{r(r-2{\cal M})}+H_{0}\Bigg\{\frac{4\pi r(\bar{p}+\bar{\rho})}{(r-2{\cal M})c_{s}^{2}}\\
-\frac{2{\cal M}r\left[-\ell(\ell+1)+52\pi r^{2}\bar{p}+20\pi r^{2}\bar{\rho}\right]+r^{2}\ell(\ell+1)+4{\cal M}^{2}+4\pi r^{4}\left[\bar{p}\left(16\pi r^{2}\bar{p}-9\right)-5\bar{\rho}\right]}{r^{2}(r-2{\cal M})^{2}}\Bigg\}\;.

(5.22)

5.2.2
Linearized axial equations

We now turn to gravito-magnetic — i.e., parity-odd (axial) — perturbations of Einstein’s equations, working in the Regge–Wheeler gauge (
h_{2}=0
), for an isotropic perfect-fluid star described by the stress–energy tensor (
5.2
). The odd equations are (see, e.g., Ref.
385
)

\displaystyle e^{-\Phi}\dot{h}_{0}+\left(\frac{2\mathcal{M}}{r}-1\right)h_{1}^{\prime}+\left(4\pi r(\bar{\rho}-\bar{p}-\frac{2\mathcal{M}}{r^{2}}\right)h_{1}

\displaystyle=0,

(5.23a)

\displaystyle e^{-\Phi}\left(\dot{h}_{0}^{\prime}-\frac{2}{r}\dot{h}_{0}-\ddot{h}_{1}\right)-\frac{(\ell-1)(\ell+2)}{r^{2}}h_{1}

\displaystyle=0,

(5.23b)

\displaystyle e^{-\Psi}\left(h_{0}^{\prime\prime}-\dot{h}_{1}^{\prime}\right)-4\pi r(\bar{p}+\bar{\rho})\left(h_{0}^{\prime}-\dot{h}_{1}\right)-\frac{2e^{-\Psi}}{r}\dot{h}_{1}

\displaystyle-\frac{\ell(\ell+1)r-4\mathcal{M}+8\pi r^{3}(\bar{p}+\bar{\rho})}{r^{3}}h_{0}-4e^{\Phi}U

\displaystyle=0.

(5.23c)

It is clear that
eq.

5.23a
can generally be solved for
h_{0}
in terms of
h_{1}
, as long as the perturbations are not exactly static. For non-static perturbations, after defining the field
\psi
as

h_{1}\equiv e^{\frac{1}{2}(\Psi-\Phi)}r\psi

(5.24)

and Fourier transforming in time, one obtains, from
eq.

5.23a
,

h_{0}=\frac{ie^{\frac{1}{2}(\Phi-\Psi)}}{\omega}\left(r\psi^{\prime}+\psi\right).

(5.25)

Plugging this into
eq.

5.23b
, one finds

e^{\frac{1}{2}(\Phi-\Psi)}\partial_{r}\left(e^{\frac{1}{2}(\Phi-\Psi)}\psi^{\prime}\right)+\left[\omega^{2}-e^{\Phi}\left(\frac{\ell(\ell+1)}{r^{2}}-\frac{6\mathcal{M}}{r^{3}}+4\pi(\bar{\rho}-\bar{p})\right)\right]\psi=0,

(5.26)

which corresponds to the Regge–Wheeler equation describing axial perturbations within the star (see, e.g., Ref.
385
). In particular, it recovers the result of Ref.
180
upon taking
\omega\rightarrow 0
.
Equation

5.23c
can finally be used to express the fluid perturbation
U
in terms of the axial metric perturbation. In Fourier space,

U=-\frac{4i\pi(\bar{p}+\bar{\rho})e^{-\frac{1}{2}(\Phi+\Psi)}}{\omega}\left(r\psi^{\prime}+\psi\right)=-4\pi e^{-\Phi}(\bar{p}+\bar{\rho})h_{0}.

(5.27)

It has been noted in the literature
180
,
64
,
242
,
494
,
493
that taking the
\omega\rightarrow 0
limit in
eq.

5.26
yields a result that is
not
equivalent to the one obtained by setting
\omega=0
from the outset. Notice that
eq.

5.26
at
\omega\neq 0
can equivalently be written as an equation for
h_{0}
by inverting the relation (
5.25
). The resulting expression is somewhat more involved, but it admits a well-defined limit as
\omega\rightarrow 0
. Taking this limit at the very end yields the equation
403
,
493

e^{-\Psi}h_{0}^{\prime\prime}-4\pi r(\bar{p}+\bar{\rho})h_{0}^{\prime}-\left[\frac{\ell(\ell+1)}{r^{2}}-\frac{4\mathcal{M}}{r^{3}}-8\pi(\bar{p}+\bar{\rho})\right]h_{0}=0.

(5.28)

This equation can be contrasted with the one obtained by setting
\omega=0
directly at the level of the Einstein equations (
5.23a
)–(
5.23c
). In the strictly static régime, defined by
\dot{h}_{0}=\dot{h}_{1}=0
and
U=0
, one finds from
eq.

5.23b
that
h_{1}=0
, and from
eq.

5.23c
that

e^{-\Psi}h_{0}^{\prime\prime}-4\pi r(\bar{p}+\bar{\rho})h_{0}^{\prime}-\left[\frac{\ell(\ell+1)}{r^{2}}-\frac{4\mathcal{M}}{r^{3}}+8\pi(\bar{p}+\bar{\rho})\right]h_{0}=0,

(5.29)

which reproduces the equation derived in Ref.
64
, where the axial perturbations of a strictly static fluid were studied.
As noted in Ref.
403
,
eq.

5.29
is very similar to
eq.

5.28
, the only difference being the opposite sign in front of the
(\bar{p}+\bar{\rho})
term.
This discrepancy can be attributed to the apparent loss of a constraint — specifically,
eq.

5.27
— when
\omega=0
is imposed from the outset.

In summary, the equation derived in Ref.
180
coincides with that used in Ref.
403
(
eq.

5.28
) for an
irrotational
fluid. This has been explained by the fact that the zero-frequency limit of the Regge–Wheeler equation enforces the fluid to be irrotational rather than strictly
static

493
. By contrast, the inequivalence between the static-limit master equations obtained in Ref.
180
(
eq.

5.28
) and Ref.
64
(
eq.

5.29
) has been attributed to the fact that the fluid is irrotational in the former case, but strictly static in the latter
493
.

This behavior is reminiscent of what occurs, in a different form, in the even-parity sector above, where the strict static limit introduces ambiguities in some of the constraints and appears discontinuous.
Note that this apparent discontinuity is absent in vacuum general relativity for black hole perturbation equations: in the absence of a matter sector,
eqs.

5.28
and
5.29
coincide when
\bar{p}=\bar{\rho}=0
.
A detailed discussion of these issues within an EFT framework for the fluid — along the lines of modern EFT approaches
216
,
217
— remains absent.

We have presented the equations describing a non-rotating star. The study of linear perturbation theory for slowly rotating stars was pioneered in Refs.
349
,
129
,
385
,
387
,
386
,
244
(see also Ref.
497
for a review). These equations were later solved to compute the tidal response in Refs.
495
,
404
. We will return to the calculation of the tidal Love numbers in
Part

III
below.

Part III
Love numbers

6
Gravitational computation of tidal response coefficients

The Love numbers can be defined within the effective field theory (EFT) or post-Newtonian (PN) frameworks, as discussed in
section

2
. However, in order to compute them, one needs to match to a general relativistic calculation in which the full Einstein equations are solved under the assumption of tidal-field boundary conditions. This section provides explicit results for Love numbers and other response coefficients from gravitational perturbation theory in the linear and nonlinear régimes, covering Schwarzschild and Kerr black holes and neutron stars in four dimensions.
We further review extensions to charged black holes, higher-dimensional black holes, black branes, black strings, and supergravity black holes, and discuss Love numbers beyond pure general relativity. Results
are summarized in
table

Dynamical tidal response

See Table

6.2

Table 2:
Summary of black hole types and representative references for tidal Love number computations in four-dimensional GR, higher dimensions, extended theories of gravity, environmental effects, and nonlinear contributions. The list includes calculations of responses for various fields (e.g., gravitational, scalar). Note that the listed references may contain only partial results and be derived under specific assumptions (such as axisymmetry, test scalar fields, etc.); we refer the reader to the main text for further discussion, and to the original references for a comprehensive account of the assumptions underlying each work.

The term “compact object” refers to an object whose radius
R_{*}
is comparable to its Schwarzschild radius
r_{\mathrm{s}}
, i.e.,
R_{*}\sim\mathcal{O}(r_{\mathrm{s}})
. This condition characterizes the strong-gravity, or UV, régime. When the compactness parameter satisfies

R_{*}/r_{\mathrm{s}}-1\ll 1
, the radial solutions of the perturbation equations (
4.78
) naturally take a characteristic asymptotic form similar to their Newtonian counterpart (
1.57
),

\phi\rightarrow\sum_{\ell=2}^{\infty}\mathcal{E}_{{\ell m}}\,r^{-s}\left[r^{\ell}\left(1+a_{1}\frac{r_{\mathrm{s}}}{r}+\dots\right)+\frac{\lambda_{\ell}}{r^{\ell+1}}\left(1+b_{1}\frac{r_{\mathrm{s}}}{r}+\dots\right)\right]+{\cal O}(\dot{{\cal E}},{\cal E}^{2}).

(6.1)

Here
\phi
is a generic proxy for a suitably normalized spin-
s
field,
91
91

91

For example, the perturbation field
\phi
can be expressed either in terms of the Newtonian potential
U
appearing in the metric perturbation

g_{tt}=-1+\frac{2U}{c^{2}}
with
s=0
, cf.
eq.

2.1
, or in terms of the Weyl scalars
\Psi_{0}
(
s=2
) and
\Psi_{4}
(
s=-2
), as used in the Teukolsky formalism. The two descriptions are related because the Weyl scalars are constructed from second derivatives of the metric perturbation. In the weak-field limit, this implies

\Psi_{0}\sim\frac{1}{c^{2}}m^{i}m^{j}\partial_{i}\partial_{j}U,\qquad\Psi_{4}\sim\frac{1}{c^{2}}\bar{m}^{i}\bar{m}^{j}\partial_{i}\partial_{j}U.

and as in
eq.

1.57
the coefficients
\mathcal{E}_{\ell m}
are constants encoding the external field,
a_{i}
and
b_{i}
are relativistic corrections determined by the Einstein equations, and we denote by
\lambda_{\ell}
the ratio of the two falloffs, which can be complex; one would usually identify the real part with the Love numbers and the imaginary part with the dissipative response coefficients, although we remind the reader that this identification should only be made after matching to an EFT or PN calculation.

This makes the computation of Love numbers a direct parallel to the Newtonian case, offering an intuitive bridge between classical and relativistic tidal responses. We look for a solution
\phi
with the appropriate boundary condition imposed at the surface of the object, and measure the static response from the falloff at infinity, cf.
eq.

6.1
. For compact objects the boundary condition is provided by matching to the interior fluid solution at the surface. For black holes the relevant boundary conditions at the horizon are regularity for static perturbations and the ingoing wave condition in the generic dynamical case. In Boyer-Lindquist coordinates
this corresponds to

\phi\sim\begin{cases}\mathrm{const.}\times(r-r_{+})^{-i\alpha_{+}},&r\to r_{+}\\
c_{1}\,r^{\ell-s}+c_{2}\,r^{-\ell-s-1},&r\to\infty\end{cases},

(6.2)

where

\alpha_{+}\equiv\frac{\beta}{2}\left(\omega-m\Omega_{\mathrm{H}}\right)\pm i\frac{s}{2},

(6.3)

with
\beta=2r_{\mathrm{s}}r_{+}/(r_{+}-r_{-})
the inverse surface gravity of the black hole defined in
eq.

3.55
.
The factor in the near-horizon solution can be written

\displaystyle(r-r_{+})^{-i\alpha_{+}}

\displaystyle=e^{-i\alpha_{+}\log(r-r_{+})}

\displaystyle\sim e^{-i(\omega-m\Omega_{\mathrm{H}})r_{*}},

(6.4)

where the tortoise coordinate for Kerr
(satisying
\Delta\,\mathrm{d}{r_{\star}}=(r^{2}+a^{2})\,\mathrm{d}r
)
is

{r_{\star}}=r+\frac{r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}}{r_{+}-r_{-}}\log\left(\frac{r}{r_{+}}-1\right)-\frac{r_{-}^{2}+a^{2}}{r_{+}-r_{-}}\log\left(\frac{r}{r_{-}}-1\right),

(6.5)

mapping the exterior black hole region
r\in(r_{+},\infty)
to the full real line
{r_{\star}}\in(-\infty,+\infty)
. This makes the ingoing/outgoing nature of the field
\phi
unambiguous: it is just given by the sign of the exponent.

At this point, it is useful to return to our discussion in
section

2.1
on a possible ambiguity in the definition of the response coefficients.
In flat space, the two independent solutions are clearly separated, and no ambiguity arises.
However, once gravitational corrections are included (
a_{i},b_{i}\neq 0
), higher-order terms in the expansion of the tidal solution will appear at the same order in
r
as terms in the response solution. Concretely, there will be an ambiguity between
a_{i+2\ell+1}
and
b_{i}
(considering
a_{0}=b_{0}=1
) as long as
2\ell+1\in\mathbb{N}
.
Different equivalent ways to deal with this issue without relying on EFT or PN matching have been adopted in the literature.
Convenient approaches include working in general spacetime dimension, where the overlap does not occur for generic values, and only at the end taking the physical limit
389
,
340
,
301
(see
section

6.4
);
treating the multipole number as a continuous parameter
\ell\in\mathbb{R}
and taking the limit
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
at the end
410
,
411
,
132
,
549
,
445
.
Finally, the ambiguity can also be resolved within effective field theory, where a systematic matching procedure cleanly separates the contributions associated with different EFT operators (see
section

2.2.1
).

Before we proceed with the presentation of Love numbers for individual cases, it is worth clarifying an important subtlety regarding the tidal
response coefficient
\lambda_{\ell}
, which is generically complex.
At first glance, one might wonder how
\operatorname{Im}(\lambda_{\ell})\neq 0
is consistent with the metric perturbation being real. In the weak field limit, the physical metric perturbation can be obtained by summing over all mode contributions and taking the real part to ensure the metric remains real. Expanding in frequency and angular modes, this can be expressed compactly as

h^{\text{phys}}_{tt}=\sum_{\ell m}\operatorname{Re}\left[R_{\ell m}(r)S_{\ell m}(\theta)e^{-i\omega t+im\varphi}\right],

(6.6)

where
R_{\ell m}(r)
are the radial mode functions and
S_{\ell m}(\theta)
are the (spin-weighted) spheroidal harmonics. The inclusion of the complex conjugate ensures that the perturbation is real-valued, as required for a physical spacetime metric.
By expanding the real part explicitly and keeping only the leading asymptotic terms of
eq.

6.1
, with
s=0
and
\phi=R_{\ell m}(r)
,
the physical static metric perturbation (up to overall real constants) can be written as
92
92

92

We stress that the asymptotic expansion (
6.7
) in powers of
r
is valid in the small-
\omega
limit, or in the near-zone approximation (
r\ll\omega^{-1}
).

\displaystyle h^{\rm physical}_{tt}

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\ell m}}\mathcal{E}_{{\ell m}}S_{\ell m}(\theta)r^{\ell}\Bigg[\left(1+a_{1}\frac{r_{s}}{r}+\cdots\right)\cos(\omega t-m\varphi)

(6.7)

\displaystyle+\left(\frac{1}{r}\right)^{2\ell+1}\left(1+b_{1}\frac{r_{s}}{r}+\cdots\right)\Big(\operatorname{Re}(\lambda_{\ell})\cos(\omega t-m\varphi)-\operatorname{Im}(\lambda_{\ell})\sin(\omega t-m\varphi)\Big)\Bigg]

\displaystyle\simeq\sum_{{\ell m}}\mathcal{E}_{{\ell m}}S_{\ell m}(\theta)r^{\ell}\Bigg[\cos(\omega t-m\varphi)+\left|\lambda_{\ell}(\omega)\right|\cos\big(\omega t-m\varphi+\delta_{\ell}(\omega)\big)\left(\frac{1}{r}\right)^{2\ell+1}\Bigg],

where
|\lambda_{\ell}|
is the magnitude and
\delta_{\ell}
is the phase of
\lambda_{\ell}
.
93
93

93

For a complex number
\lambda=a+ib
, we can write it in exponential (polar) form
\lambda=|\lambda|\,e^{i\delta}
, where the magnitude and phase are defined as
|\lambda|=\sqrt{a^{2}+b^{2}}
and the phase is the angle with respect to the real axis,
\delta=\arctan(b/a)
taken in
(-\pi,\,\pi]
. Note also that

\operatorname{Re}(\lambda_{\ell})\cos\theta-\operatorname{Im}(\lambda_{\ell})\sin\theta=|\lambda_{\ell}|\left(\cos\delta_{\ell}\cos\theta-\sin\delta_{\ell}\sin\theta\right)=|\lambda_{\ell}|\cos(\theta+\delta_{\ell}).

We see that
\operatorname{Im}(\lambda_{\ell})
when
\omega\neq 0
produces a time delay in additon to an azimuthal phase lag in
\varphi
between the tidal forcing and the metric response. The factor
\left(1/r\right)^{2\ell+1}
acts as a multipole filter at large
r
. To be more precise, considering the
\ell=2
quadrupole which falls as
\left(1/r\right)^{5}
to dominate at large
r
,
the
\ell=3
octupole falls as
\left(1/r\right)^{7}
and becomes negligible. Higher
\ell
are exponentially irrelevant at large
separations. The observable metric at large
r
is therefore dominated by the lowest
allowed
\ell
, which for a symmetric tidal environment is
\ell=2
— this is why
gravitational wave models need only a handful of multipoles.

The complexity of
\lambda_{\ell}(\omega)
is therefore
a feature of the mode decomposition, not of the metric itself, and its real
and imaginary parts carry distinct physical information. The reality condition
on the metric translates into the following constraint on the tidal response
coefficient

\lambda_{\ell\,-m}(-\omega)=\lambda_{\ell m}^{*}(\omega)

(6.8)

which can be verified directly.

For
\omega=0
the
\operatorname{Im}(\lambda_{\ell})
term in
eq.

6.7
produces an azimuthal phase lag

\sim\sin(m\varphi)
— the metric response is rotated in
\varphi
relative to
the tidal source by
\pi/(2m)
. This is the static manifestation of frame-dragging: even
though the external perturbation is time-independent in the inertial frame,
the black hole’s rotation twists the metric response azimuthally. This effect has a concrete observational consequence in binary systems: when a small body orbits a rotating black hole, the tidal distortion of the larger body would be slightly twisted relative to the orbital plane. This contributes a measurable phase shift in the gravitational waveform that, in principle, allows measurement of the imaginary tidal Love numbers — a direct probe of horizon dissipation that LISA-band sources may constrain.

6.1
Static tidal response

Our aim in this section is to begin with the simplest setups and progressively introduce various bells and whistles. We will start off by describing the tidal responses for Schwarzschild and Kerr black holes in a purely static (time-independent) external environment in linear perturbation theory, showing that the Love numbers vanish for all four-dimensional black holes. We expand the discussion to include dynamical tidal responses in
section

6.2
and nonlinear tidal responses in
section

6.3

6.1.1
Schwarzschild black holes

Starting with the Schwarzschild solution as our background, we study linear perturbations describing the black hole in an external environment, encoding its Love numbers. This simplified setting allows us to clearly introduce the relevant framework and boundary conditions before moving on to the Kerr background, where the radial equations are more involved and the extraction of the corresponding Love numbers is correspondingly more subtle.

As a first step, we focus on the massless scalar, whose radial equation is well known and provides a simple and transparent illustration of the method.
While gravitational perturbations are intrinsically spin-2, the spin-0 case retains the essential radial structure of the gravitational perturbation equations and therefore serves as a useful “toy model” for understanding how an external tidal field elicits a response from a black hole.
We employ this toy model for several reasons. First, it is technically simpler and allows us to present the method in a transparent way. Second, the gravitational problem is so closely analogous that most of the main qualitative conclusions still hold. Third, the spin-0 equation of motion is directly shared by a number of gravitational master variables of interest, including the Kaluza–Klein field
\phi=\ln(-g_{tt})
in four dimensions
157
(cf.
section

6.3
) and the master field governing tensor-mode metric perturbations in higher spacetime dimensions

D>4

384
,
352
(cf.
section

6.4.2
).

Scalar field

Recall that the Klein–Gordon equation
\Box\phi=0
expanded in spherical harmonics is (
3.33
)

\partial_{r}\left(\Delta\partial_{r}\phi\right)+\frac{\omega^{2}r^{4}}{\Delta}\phi-\ell(\ell+1)\phi=0.

(6.9)

We remind the reader of the definitions
\Delta=r(r-r_{\mathrm{s}})=r^{2}f(r)
and
f(r)=1-r/r_{\mathrm{s}}
, where
r_{\mathrm{s}}=2GM
is the Schwarzschild radius.
To compute the static tidal response we may ignore time derivatives by setting
\omega=0
(cf.
section

2
),

\partial_{r}\left(\Delta\partial_{r}\phi\right)-\ell(\ell+1)\phi=0.

(6.10)

It is convenient to redefine the radial variable as

x\equiv\frac{\Delta^{\prime}}{r_{\mathrm{s}}}=\frac{2r}{r_{\mathrm{s}}}-1,

(6.11)

after which
eq.

6.10
reveals itself to be Legendre’s differential equation,

\partial_{x}\left[(1-x^{2})\partial_{x}\phi\right]+\ell(\ell+1)\phi=0.

(6.12)

This equation and its solutions are discussed in detail in
A.2
. The solutions are the Legendre functions of the first and second kind,

\phi=c_{1}P_{\ell}(x)+c_{2}Q_{\ell}(x).

(6.13)

Asymptotically at infinity and the horizon they behave like

\displaystyle P_{\ell}(x)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\begin{cases}\frac{(2\ell)!}{\ell!^{2}}\left(\frac{r}{r_{\mathrm{s}}}\right)^{\ell},&r\to\infty\\
1,&r\to r_{\mathrm{s}}\end{cases},

(6.14a)

\displaystyle Q_{\ell}(x)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\begin{cases}\frac{\ell!(\ell+1)!}{(2\ell+2)!}\left(\frac{r_{\mathrm{s}}}{r}\right)^{\ell+1},&r\to\infty\\
-\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(\frac{r}{r_{\mathrm{s}}}-1\right)-H_{\ell},&r\to r_{\mathrm{s}}\end{cases},

(6.14b)

where
H_{n}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}k^{-1}
are the harmonic numbers. These values of
r
have straightforward asymptotics because (along with
r=0
) they are two of the three regular singular points of
eq.

6.12
.

To fix the solution (
6.13
) we must impose two boundary conditions. One is the overall amplitude at infinity
\mathcal{E}_{\ell m}
, cf.
eq.

6.1
, which does not affect the computation of the tidal response
\lambda_{\ell}
. The other boundary condition is typically set at the surface of the object, which for a black hole we take to be the horizon. For a compact object such as a white dwarf or neutron star, this boundary condition is determined by the object’s internal fluid dynamics, and is generally an admixture of the
P_{\ell}(x)
and
Q_{\ell}(x)
solutions.
For a black hole, we note that
Q_{\ell}(x)
diverges at the horizon. Imposing regularity of the solution, as required by the boundary condition (
6.2
), selects the regular branch and therefore sets

c_{2}=0.

(6.15)

To calculate the Love number, we need to follow the solution
\phi(x)=c_{1}P_{\ell}(x)
from the surface of the object to infinity, where (cf.
eq.

6.1
) we read off the response coefficient from the coefficient in front of the decaying mode. From
eq.

6.14
we see that the growing solution (corresponding to the external tidal field) at infinity lives in
P_{\ell}(x)
, while the decaying solution (the induced response) is in
Q_{\ell}(x)
. Indeed, for integer values of
\ell
the Legendre functions of the first kind
P_{\ell}(x)
are more commonly known as Legendre polynomials because they are just polynomials in
x
, lacking any
1/r
tails. As a result we find that the static, scalar Love numbers of Schwarzschild black holes vanish,

\lambda^{s=0}_{\mathrm{Sch,BH}}=0.

(6.16)

Around a compact object the static response generally does not vanish, as there is typically no reason to expect the internal dynamics of the star to yield zero contribution from
Q_{\ell}(x)
at the surface. Following the same logic as for the black hole, the tidal response is
c_{2}/c_{1}
(which can be determined just from the matching at the surface).
This contrast underscores a fundamental difference between black holes and ordinary celestial objects: whereas planets and stars respond to tidal forces with measurable deformations, Schwarzschild black holes are uniquely insensitive to such external influences. This feature has significant implications for gravitational-wave physics and black hole modeling.

Gravitational perturbations

The generalization to gravitational perturbations is obtained by solving the Einstein equations in the static limit for linearized metric fluctuations. The calculation of the relativistic Love numbers of Schwarzschild black holes in general relativity was pioneered independently by Damour & Nagar
180
and Binnington & Poisson
64
.

As we saw in
section

4.1.3
, the metric perturbations
h_{0}
and
H_{0}
are (up to rescalings) the natural master variables in the static limit
241
,
333
,
180
,
64
.
Their static equations of motion are
eqs.

4.44
and
4.55
,

\displaystyle\Delta H_{0}^{\prime\prime}+\Delta^{\prime}H_{0}^{\prime}-\frac{r_{\mathrm{s}}^{2}+\ell(\ell+1)\Delta}{\Delta}H_{0}

\displaystyle=0,

(6.17)

\displaystyle h_{0}^{\prime\prime}+\frac{4}{r}h_{0}^{\prime}-\frac{(\ell+2)(\ell-1)}{\Delta}h_{0}=0,

(6.18)

where primes denote
\partial_{r}
.

Both of these are hypergeometric differential equations, which we discuss in detail in
A
. Like the Legendre equation (
6.12
) for the scalar field, which is itself a special case of the hypergeometric class, it possesses three regular singular points located at
r=(0,r_{\mathrm{s}},\infty)
(indeed the hypergeometric equation is the general second-order ordinary differential equation with three regular and no irregular singularities). The odd equation (
6.18
) is already in hypergeometric form (
A.2
) while the even equation (
6.17
) takes this form acting on
\Delta H_{0}
. The parameters in each case are

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{c})_{\mathrm{even}}

\displaystyle=(\ell+3,2-\ell,3),

(6.19a)

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{c})_{\mathrm{odd}}

\displaystyle=(\ell+2,1-\ell,4),

(6.19b)

although for the even equation it is simpler to notice that (after changing to the variable
x=\Delta^{\prime}/r_{\mathrm{s}}
but without rescaling
H_{0}
) it takes the form of the associated Legendre equation (
A.19
) with
m=2
. This puts the (even-parity) spin-2 field
H_{0}
on the same footing as the spin-0 field
\phi
, as both radial profiles satisfy the associated Legendre equation with
m=|s|
.
Using the asymptotics of the hypergeometric function listed in
A.1.2
, we find the horizon-regular solutions,

\displaystyle H_{0}(r)

\displaystyle\propto P^{2}_{\ell}(\Delta^{\prime}/r_{\mathrm{s}}),

(6.20a)

\displaystyle h_{0}(r)

\displaystyle\propto{}_{2}F_{1}(1-\ell,\ell+2;4;r/r_{\mathrm{s}}),

(6.20b)

where
P_{\ell}^{m}
is the associated Legendre polynomial and
{}_{2}F_{1}
is the hypergeometric function.
and
\mathcal{E}^{(E/B)}_{\ell m}
are constants corresponding to the asymptotic tidal field amplitudes, cf.
eq.

2.1
.
Because the parameter
\mathfrak{a}
in both hypergeometric equations is a negative integer, the hypergeometric series (
A.3
) truncates to a finite polynomial (
A.9
). As in the spin-0 case, our horizon-regular solutions are therefore pure polynomials in
r
, and we again infer the vanishing of the static tidal coefficients (see
eq.

2.1
):

k_{\ell}^{(\mathrm{E})}=k_{\ell}^{(\mathrm{B})}=0.

(6.21)

The gauge-invariant Love numbers, determined, for instance, by matching with the EFT as described in
section

2.2
(see, e.g.,
eq.

2.126
),
therefore vanish identically for Schwarzschild black holes.

The gravitational computation can also be performed with the various master equations we have found, namely the Zerilli (
4.29
), Regge–Wheeler (
4.41
), and Teukolsky equations (
4.78
) (the last of which we will study on Kerr in the next section). Of these, only the Zerilli equation is not hypergeometric in the static limit (it is of the more general Heun type), and it can be made hypergeometric using the Chandrasekhar transformation (
4.58
). All of these computations again yield vanishing Love numbers
389
,
340
,
151
, as we would expect since the differential operators relating these quantities to the metric perturbations do not change the Love number boundary conditions.

6.1.2
Kerr black holes

Black hole rotation enriches the tidal response with new features, and a thorough understanding of the corresponding static Love numbers is essential to quantify how angular momentum modifies the deformability of the horizon and its induced multipole moments.
As summarized in
section

2.2.2
, the coupling between the object’s angular momentum and the external tidal field introduces new families of rotational tidal response coefficients.
94
94

94

The geometry of a tidally deformed black hole in the small-rotation limit and at higher orders was derived in Refs.
657
,
487
,
528
.
Tidal deformations of slowly-spinning objects were studied in Refs.
528
,
404
,
495
(see also Refs.
494
,
405
,
259
), which found that the Love numbers of a black hole vanish through quadratic order in spin in the axisymmetric sector.
These results were placed on a firmer basis more recently by Refs.
411
,
410
, who computed the tidal response of a Kerr black hole in full generality and showed that the static Love numbers (i.e., the conservative tidal response coefficients) vanish for all multipole moments. As remarked in Ref.
151
and independently studied in Refs.
273
,
132
in the language of point-particle EFT, the non-vanishing imaginary terms in the induced falloffs found in Refs.
411
,
410
correspond to a dissipative tidal response, often referred to as tidal heating
313
,
339
,
524
.
The vanishing of the conservative response was later interpreted from the perspective of hidden symmetries of the Kerr perturbations in Refs.
341
,
342
,
131
,
133
, which we review in more detail in
section

7
.

In the following, we briefly review the computation of the static response coefficients for spin-
s
perturbations of Kerr black holes in general relativity.

The radial Teukolsky equation (
4.78
) in the static limit is (setting
G=1
)

\left[\Delta^{-s}\partial_{r}\left(\Delta^{s+1}\partial_{r}\right)+\frac{a^{2}m^{2}+2isam(r-M)}{\Delta}-\ell(\ell+1)+s(s+1)\right]R^{(s)}_{\ell m\omega}(r)=0,

(6.22)

where

\Delta=r(r-2M)+a^{2}=(r-r_{+})(r-r_{-}),\qquad r_{\pm}=M\pm\sqrt{M^{2}-a^{2}}.

(6.23)

Here
a
is the Kerr spin parameter (with dimensions of length), satisfying
0<a<M
for a non-extremal black hole.
For compactness we write
\phi\equiv R^{(s)}_{\ell m\omega}(r)
in this section.

Equation

6.22
exhibits regular singular points at
r=r_{\pm}
, corresponding to the outer and inner horizons, and at
r=\infty
. Near the horizons the solution behaves as a power law,

\phi\sim(r-r_{\pm})^{\alpha_{\pm}},

(6.24)

with indicial exponents

\alpha_{\pm}=-\frac{s}{2}\pm im\gamma,\qquad\gamma=\frac{a}{r_{+}-r_{-}}.

(6.25)

At spatial infinity one finds solutions going as

\phi(r)\sim r^{\ell-s}\quad\text{or}\quad r^{-(\ell+1+s)},

(6.26)

corresponding respectively to the applied tidal field and the induced multipole response.

Because the equation possesses exactly three regular singular points, it can be mapped to the Gauss hypergeometric equation. To achieve this, introduce the dimensionless coordinate

z=\frac{r-r_{+}}{r-r_{-}},

(6.27)

so that the outer horizon is at
z=0
, the inner horizon at
z=\infty
, and spatial infinity at
z=1
. We further redefine

\phi(r)=(r-r_{-})^{p}(r-r_{+})^{q}w(z),

(6.28)

with exponents chosen as

p=-\frac{s}{2}-im\gamma,\qquad q=-\frac{s}{2}+im\gamma.

(6.29)

In terms of
z
,
eq.

6.22
takes the hypergeometric form (
A.2
) with parameters

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}

\displaystyle=1+\ell-s,

(6.30a)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{b}

\displaystyle=1+\ell+2im\gamma,

(6.30b)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{c}

\displaystyle=1-s+2im\gamma.

(6.30c)

As in the Schwarzschild case, the parameters are degenerate,

\mathfrak{c}-\mathfrak{a}-\mathfrak{b}=-2\ell-1\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0},

(6.31)

which implies that
\mathfrak{c}-\mathfrak{a}-\mathfrak{b}
is a negative integer.
The general solution is the linear combination

\displaystyle w(z)={}

\displaystyle d_{1}\,{}_{2}F_{1}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b};\mathfrak{c};z)+d_{2}\,z^{1-\mathfrak{c}}\,{}_{2}F_{1}(\mathfrak{a}-\mathfrak{c}+1,\mathfrak{b}-\mathfrak{c}+1;2-\mathfrak{c};z),

(6.32)

with arbitrary constants
d_{1},d_{2}
.
We impose boundary conditions at the horizon and at infinity. Regularity on the future horizon
r=r_{+}
requires that the solution be analytic at
z=0
, which selects the branch proportional to
{}_{2}F_{1}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b};\mathfrak{c};z)
, i.e.,
d_{2}=0
.
The full solution is then obtained by analytically continuing this branch to
z\to 1
(i.e.,
r\to\infty
), where it becomes a linear combination of the two independent asymptotic behaviors,

\displaystyle{}_{2}F_{1}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b};\mathfrak{c};z)={}

\displaystyle\frac{\Gamma(\mathfrak{c})\Gamma(\mathfrak{c}-\mathfrak{a}-\mathfrak{b})}{\Gamma(\mathfrak{c}-\mathfrak{a})\Gamma(\mathfrak{c}-\mathfrak{b})}\,{}_{2}F_{1}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b};\mathfrak{a}+\mathfrak{b}-\mathfrak{c}+1;1-z)

(6.33)

\displaystyle+(1-z)^{\mathfrak{c}-\mathfrak{a}-\mathfrak{b}}\frac{\Gamma(\mathfrak{c})\Gamma(\mathfrak{a}+\mathfrak{b}-\mathfrak{c})}{\Gamma(\mathfrak{a})\Gamma(\mathfrak{b})}\,{}_{2}F_{1}(\mathfrak{c}-\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{c}-\mathfrak{b};\mathfrak{c}-\mathfrak{a}-\mathfrak{b}+1;1-z).

(6.34)

Substituting the solution (
6.32
) with
d_{2}=0
into
eq.

6.28
and using the asymptotic expansion (
6.34
), we obtain the large-
r
behavior

\phi(r)\sim r^{\ell-s}\left(c_{1}+c_{2}r^{-(2\ell+1)}\right),

(6.35)

which allows the static tidal response to be computed as

\displaystyle\lambda_{\ell}^{\rm Kerr}

\displaystyle=\frac{c_{2}}{c_{1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma(\mathfrak{a})\Gamma(\mathfrak{b})\Gamma(\mathfrak{c}-\mathfrak{a}-\mathfrak{b})}{\Gamma(\mathfrak{c}-\mathfrak{a})\Gamma(\mathfrak{c}-\mathfrak{b})\Gamma(\mathfrak{a}+\mathfrak{b}-\mathfrak{c})}(r_{+}-r_{-})^{2\ell+1}.

(6.36)

Substituting the parameters (
6.30
) and simplifying yields
95
95

95

Although the individual gamma functions on the first line diverge for negative integer arguments, the poles cancel exactly between the numerator and denominator, leading to the finite final result. We have written the second line as it usually appears in the literature, but note that the product can be expressed using the Pochhammer symbol,
(a)_{n}=\Gamma(a+n)/\Gamma(a)
, as

\prod_{n=1}^{\ell}\left(n^{2}+4m^{2}\gamma^{2}\right)=(1+2im\gamma)_{\ell}(1-2im\gamma)_{\ell}.

\displaystyle\lambda_{\ell}^{\rm Kerr}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma(-2\ell-1)\,\Gamma(1+\ell-s)\,\Gamma(1+\ell+2im\gamma)}{\Gamma(-\ell-s)\,\Gamma(-\ell+2im\gamma)\,\Gamma(2\ell+1)}(r_{+}-r_{-})^{2\ell+1}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{s+1}im\gamma\frac{(\ell+s)!(\ell-s)!}{(2\ell+1)!\,(2\ell)!}(r_{+}-r_{-})^{2\ell+1}\prod_{n=1}^{\ell}\left(n^{2}+4m^{2}\gamma^{2}\right).

(6.37)

This is imaginary, establishing that the static tidal response of Kerr black holes is purely dissipative in nature
411
,
410
.

Several key features follow immediately. First, despite the presence of rotation, all static electric- and magnetic-type Love numbers vanish identically for non-extremal Kerr black holes in classical vacuum GR. This result holds for arbitrary spin
a<M
and for all multipoles
\ell\geq 2
. Physically, imposing regularity at the event horizon removes the real part of the decaying (response) mode and leaves only the externally applied tidal field and an imaginary (dissipative) response. Consequently, Kerr black holes do not develop induced multipole moments under static tidal deformations, reinforcing their no-hair-like character and sharply distinguishing them from material compact objects such as neutron stars. Second, the coefficients (
6.37
) are purely imaginary, depend on the azimuthal number
m
while coupling to the Kerr black hole spin through the product
m\gamma
and only involve odd powers of
m\gamma
. Third, the large-
\ell
asymptotic behavior of
eq.

6.37
takes the simple form, for a generic black hole spin
a
,

\lambda^{\rm Kerr}_{\ell}\sim(-1)^{s+1}\frac{\ell}{2^{4\ell+2}}i\sinh(2\pi m\gamma)\quad\text{as }\ell\to\infty\,,

(6.38)

demonstrating that the linear response of the black hole is exponentially suppressed at large multipolar order. Fourth, analytic continuation shows that in the Schwarzschild limit
(a\to 0)
one recovers

\lim_{a\rightarrow 0}\lambda^{\rm Kerr}_{\ell}=\lambda_{\ell}^{\mathrm{Sch}}=0.

(6.39)

Figure 5:
Static dissipative response coefficients (
6.37
) for Kerr black holes

k_{\ell}^{\mathrm{Kerr}}=r_{\mathrm{s}}^{-2\ell-1}\lambda_{\ell}^{\mathrm{Kerr}}

for gravitational fields
s=2
as a function of the dimensionless angular momentum
J/M^{2}
for
\ell=2,4
. The endpoints of these curves correspond to extremal black holes, which are maximally rotating and have zero temperature.

The dimensionless static dissipative coefficients

k_{\ell}^{\mathrm{Kerr}}=r_{\mathrm{s}}^{-2\ell-1}\lambda^{\rm Kerr}_{\ell}

are shown in
fig.

5
as a function of the dimensionless spin
J/M^{2}
, for gravitational (
s=2
) perturbations at
\ell=2
and
\ell=4
. All curves vanish at
\chi=0
(Schwarzschild) and grow monotonically with spin, reaching either the maximum at extremality or at an intermediate point before decreasing to a finite, non-zero value in the extremal limit
a/M\to 1
. This endpoint behavior, corresponding to a maximally rotating Kerr black hole, was first noted in Refs.
411
,
410
and has recently been computed explicitly using the Leaver–MST method
516
.

The full general-relativistic solution (
6.35
) with response coefficients (
6.36
) for a Kerr black hole can be mapped onto the language of point-particle EFT, following the matching procedure reviewed in
section

2.3
. This has been done explicitly in Refs.
273
,
132
. In the notation of
eq.

2.84
(following Ref.
572
), one finds
273
,
132
,
572

\Lambda^{(\mathrm{E},\mathrm{B})}_{\omega^{0},S^{0}}=\Lambda^{(\mathrm{E},\mathrm{B})}_{\omega^{0},S^{2}}=\Lambda^{(\mathrm{E},\mathrm{B})}_{\omega^{0},S^{4}}=0,

(6.40)

for the static Love numbers, and (in units of
-M(GM)^{4}
)

H^{(\mathrm{E},\mathrm{B})}_{\omega^{0},S^{1}}=-\frac{8}{45}(\chi+3\chi^{3}),\qquad H^{(\mathrm{E},\mathrm{B})}_{\omega^{0},S^{3}}=\frac{2}{3}\chi^{3},

(6.41)

for the static dissipation numbers.
Here
\chi
is the dimensionless spin parameter defined by
J\equiv\chi GM^{2}=\sqrt{(1/2)S^{ij}S_{ij}}
, with
J
the spin magnitude and
S_{ij}
the spin tensor
572
.

The computation of static tidal Love numbers has been extended to fermionic perturbations in Ref.
121
. This work highlights a distinction between the (static) bosonic and fermionic solutions to the Teukolsky equation, suggesting that fermionic Love numbers of a Kerr black hole are non-zero, while their corresponding dissipation numbers vanish, contrary to the integer-spin results. A later extension to Reissner–Nordström black holes is discussed in Ref.
491
.

6.2
Dynamical tidal response

The tidal Love numbers discussed in the preceding sections are defined in the static limit
\omega=0
, characterizing the adiabatic deformation of a compact object in a time-independent external field. In a realistic binary, however, the tidal field varies on the orbital timescale, and one must consider the
dynamical
tidal response at finite driving frequency
\omega
. A comprehensive, if not exhaustive, classification of the literature on
dynamical tidal deformations — spanning approximately fifteen years of
developments — is provided in
table

3
.

Dynamical tides
